Wick Renormalized Parabolic Stochastic Quantization Equations on Rough Metric Measure Spaces
Hongyi Chen, Yifan (Johnny) Yang

Abstract
On metric measure spaces with sub-Gaussian heat kernel behavior in small time, we obtain sufficient conditions for the well-posedness of Wick renormalized stochastic quantization equations with polynomial interaction. Given the power of the nonlinearity, the local solution condition depends on the Hausdorff dimension d h d_{h} , the walk dimension d w d_{w} , and the maximal spatial Hölder regularity Θ \Theta of the heat kernel. Global solutions exist under a slightly more restrictive condition on the same parameters, corresponding to the full range in which the a priori energy argument of [ 91 , 109 ] can be carried out. For all global solutions, we construct an invariant measure for the Markov process defined by the solution. Our results apply to many rough spaces such as Barlow–Kigami type fractals as well as their Cartesian products and open up the possibility of making rigorous various structures in quantum field theory and statistical mechanics in non-integer dimensions. In the process, we build entirely from the short-time heat semigroup the necessary analytic framework that accommodates the issues which come with allowing rough local geometry.



1
Introduction

For
n\geq 3
odd, we seek to develop a solution theory for the
\Phi^{n+1}
equations, formally written as

\partial_{t}\phi=-L\phi-\phi-\phi^{n}+\xi,

(1.1)

where
\xi
is a Gaussian space-time noise,
L
is a self-adjoint, non-negative operator, in a general setting where renormalization is required. In the Euclidean setting,
L
is some diffusion operator, usually the Laplacian
-\Delta
, and
\xi
is space-time white noise. Both of these can be defined on certain metric measure spaces via the theory of Dirichlet forms pioneered by Fukushima
[
52
]
. This paper will provide some sufficient conditions, in terms of heat kernel properties, such that a renormalized (local/global) solution theory exists for (
1.1
) as well as its invariant measures on those spaces (see Theorem
1.4
).

Of course, (
1.1
) is only formal as written and giving rigorous meaning to it can be challenging. Its solution theory, even locally-in-time, is non-trivial and requires “renormalization” when “dimension” is bigger than or equal to 2, due to the singularity of the non-linear term
\phi^{n}
. Stochastic equations which require renormalization are known as singular stochastic partial differential equations (singular SPDEs).

Equation (
1.1
) has connections to quantum field theory and statistical mechanics. In quantum field theory, equation (
1.1
) is the Langevin dynamic of the formal Gibbs measure

\mu(d\phi)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\int_{M}\Bigl[\tfrac{1}{2}(\phi L\phi+\phi^{2})+\frac{1}{n+1}\phi^{n+1}\Bigr]\,d\mu\Bigr)D\phi,

where
``D\phi"
is the non-existent Lebesgue measure over some infinite-dimensional function space (see
[
99
]
). In this context, (
1.1
) is known as the “stochastic quantization equation”. The rigorous construction of this Gibbs measure in dimensions 2 and 3 were crowning achievements of constructive quantum field theory in the 1970s, and they still remain the source of fruitful theoretical exploration. For this reason, the study of the solution of (
1.1
) and its invariant measure remains an active topic in low dimensional Euclidean spaces
[
20
,
59
,
67
,
91
,
103
,
102
,
109
]
. In recent years, stochastic quantization has been used to study ergodic problems involving the defocusing Schrodinger equation
[
25
]
. The invariant measure has also been identified as the semiclassical limit of Gibbs states of some many-body quantum systems (see
[
96
]
and the references therein). In statistical mechanics, the solution to (
1.1
) in the case
n=3
has also been identified as scaling limits of finite range interaction Ising models at critical temperature
[
22
,
51
,
53
,
90
,
54
,
1
]
. This is believed to be one manifestation of a not fully explored universality class. In infinite volume
d\in\set{2,3}
, the fact that
\Phi^{4}
measures exist only for sufficiently small coupling corresponds to the uniqueness of equilibrium Gibbs measures of the nearest neighbor Ising model only at sufficiently high temperature
[
105
]
.

Calculations which “treat dimension as a continuum”
[
112
,
113
]
in the physics renormalization group literature predict phase transitions not visible in integer dimensions. Recently, this has motivated the stochastic quantization community to study a version of (
1.1
) where
L
is a fractional Laplacian on Euclidean spaces
[
26
,
45
,
33
]
. It is believed that since the underlying space operator is no longer local, this equation and its invariant measure belong to the universality class of certain long-range Ising models with infinite interaction radius on lattices.

This paper seeks to start a program to realize these phenomena directly on spaces with non-integer dimensions using stochastic quantization. Motivated by how physics predicts spectral dimension to govern renormalization
[
112
,
113
,
2
,
43
]
, we look to the setting of metric measure spaces where spectral dimension enters naturally via short time heat kernel behavior and strongly local Dirichlet forms. This naturally includes many rough spaces, such as fractals studied by Barlow and Kigami
[
13
,
80
]
.
Coincidentally, independent motivations from physics has led to rich theoretical explorations of constructing quantum field theories on fractal spacetimes
[
37
,
43
]
. With the exception of
[
47
]
, who worked using hierarchical models in low spectral dimension, there is no non-perturbative literature in this direction. Attempts to study the Ising model on fractal-like graphs have also been made, mostly in the physics literature
[
100
]
. These works suggest a broader program of understanding interacting field theories and statistical mechanics on irregular geometries is yet to be developed, and the present work provides a first step in this direction via stochastic quantization.

Since our central objects of study are singular SPDEs, we give a brief overview of the literature in this area. On the
d-
dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
where
L=-\Delta
, (
1.1
) requires renormalization in dimensions
d\geq 2
for any nontrivial solution theory. It is regarded as one of the simplest singular SPDEs, as it was the focus of
[
39
]
which provided a probabilistically strong solution theory
d=2
. The field experienced breakthroughs with the advent of regularity structures
[
68
]
and Paracontrolled Calculus
[
60
]
, which provided a rigorous solution theory for (
1.1
) in
d=3
for
n=3
. Since then, further techniques involving renormalization group concepts
[
46
,
19
,
61
]
to study (
1.1
) and/or its invariant measure have also emerged.

Regularity structures
[
68
]
is a local expansion-based theory and has been lauded for its wide applicability in locally Euclidean spaces. One of its most celebrated recent results is the local theory for the non-Abelian Stochastic Yang-Mills Higgs equations on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
[
32
]
, which is closely connected to the Yang-Mills millennium problem. In recent years, the theory has been extended to manifold domains in a series of works
[
41
,
89
,
65
]
. Notably, all of these seem tied to the locally Euclidean structure of the space (for example,
[
65
]
relies on the concept of jet bundles). This is natural, as regularity structures is heuristically a generalization of Taylor expansion. Since such structures do not exist on generic metric spaces, we believe that re-building this theory in our setting will need new ideas that are not in the current literature.

On the other hand, paracontrolled calculus
[
60
]
is based on harmonic analysis
[
9
]
, and is thus readily applicable in the framework of existing function space theory, as opposed to Regularity structures, which must build all of the function spaces and estimates “by hand”. For (
1.1
), global well-posedness was shown for
\mathbb{T}^{2}
first in
[
39
]
, then without reliance on invariant measures
[
91
,
109
]
(see
[
107
]
for the latest development in an exotic 2-dimensional setting). Then in
[
59
]
, paracontrolled calculus was used to give a global solution on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Since then, paracontrolled calculus has been the tool to generate global solutions for many more complicated equations in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, including vector-valued and gauge-theoretic equations
[
23
,
24
,
63
,
104
,
72
]
.

In
[
10
]
, many of the key results in
[
60
]
were reproduced in the setting of a metric measure spaces with small-time Gaussian heat kernel upper bounds. Notably, these spaces include all Riemannian and sub-Riemannian manifolds, as well as all regular (metric) graphs and their Cartesian products. A notable aspect of this work is that Besov spaces were defined only using the short time behavior of the heat kernel and all of the results did not assume (as one familiar with
[
50
]
may expect) a group structure on the underlying space or the need for some kind of Bernstein lemma (
[
9
, Section 2.1.1]
, see
[
16
, Section 2.4]
for a nontrivial geometric context). On many rough metric measure spaces, such as the Sierpinski gasket, one can obtain the precise upper and lower heat kernel estimates. In light of this, the heat semi-group approach of
[
10
]
seems to be a perfect setting to study singular (S)PDEs on general spaces from the paracontrolled perspective. One informal goal of this paper is to give evidence for the possibility of re-building paracontrolled calculus on certain rough metric spaces.

While singular SPDEs have not been studied on rough metric measure spaces, the literature on SPDEs without renormalization on general metric spaces has been growing for more than a decade. Besides the abstract functional analytic formulations
[
40
]
, the earliest results trace back to the Dirichlet form approaches in
[
74
]
, and the semi-group approaches using heat kernel estimates in
[
75
,
77
]
. The well-posedness and regularity of solutions to parabolic-type SPDEs on post critically finite fractals were studied in
[
69
,
70
,
73
]
, as well as stochastic wave equations in
[
71
]
. For metric graphs, we refer to
[
28
,
29
,
31
,
30
,
48
]
. In
[
15
]
, whose setting is close to ours, the authors studied random field solutions to the multiplicative stochastic heat equation on spaces with sub-Gaussian heat kernels. In an upcoming work, Zhenyao Sun, Louis Fan and the second author have developed a framework for SPDEs with random field solutions on general metric measure spaces and studied their comparison principles.

To the best of our knowledge, there is currently no literature on equations which require renormalization on general metric measure spaces. This article is the first step in filling that gap, using the growing theory of heat kernel based Besov spaces
[
108
,
56
,
86
,
3
,
4
,
6
,
5
]
. However, the existing literature on such spaces is mostly focused on the structure of the heat flow and function spaces themselves, and does not provide the nonlinear analytic tools required for the study of singular stochastic PDE. In particular, fundamental ingredients such as Schauder-type estimates, paraproduct decompositions, and precise connections between the Dirichlet form and useful notions of Besov-type norms are largely absent in this setting.

We discovered that reconstructing this analytic structure forces us to confront difficulties arising from allowing fractal geometry. Indeed, in Euclidean-type settings, smooth functions form an algebra and Fourier analytic tools provide fine localization. Such structures are known to not exist on general metric measure spaces. As a result, the formulation of nonlinear operations becomes delicate. These difficulties are closely related to phenomena observed in the Dirichlet forms and fractal analysis literature. In particular, the space of “smooth” test functions typically are not algebras
[
21
]
, and “derivatives” are often singular with respect to the reference measure
[
79
]
, leading to a lack of standard harmonic analytic tools such as Bernstein-type inequalities. For the study of (
1.1
), these also make certain PDE type arguments, such as the maximum principle arguments of
[
58
]
, difficult to implement here.

One of the main contributions of this paper is to show that, despite these limitations, it is possible to develop a sufficiently rich analytic framework to treat renormalized stochastic quantization equations via the Da Prato–Debussche method
[
39
]
on such spaces. This is achieved by working entirely within a heat-semigroup-based Besov framework and exploiting the available Hölder regularity of the heat kernel. From the PDE viewpoint, it is also worth mentioning that we used an energy argument to obtain the global solution to a nonlinear equation, despite all the difficulties caused by rough geometry. In this way, we obtain a clearer understanding of how rough geometry gives rise to analytic difficulties and what structures can be used to overcome them in the study of nonlinear equations. We believe these difficulties will also prohibit a general extension of our results to Sobolev settings similar to the type discussed
[
10
, Appendix B]
. For an exploration of the bad behavior of Sobolev spaces in a particular rough geometry when studying a PDE, we refer the reader to
[
101
]
, which studied the nonlinear Schrodinger equation on the Sierpinski gasket.

1.1
Assumptions and Main Results

Throughout the paper
(M,d)
denotes a locally compact complete metric space
and
\mu
a Radon measure on
M
with full support. We refer to the triple

(M,d,\mu)
as a
metric measure space
. Its diameter is

\mathrm{diam}(M):=\max_{x,y\in M}d(x,y).

For simplicity, and in order to avoid weighted function
spaces, we assume throughout that
(M,d)
is
compact
. Without loss
of generality we also take
\mathrm{diam}(M)\geq 1
and
\mu(M)\geq 1
.

We write
\mathcal{C}_{M}
for the space of continuous functions on
M
and, for

p\geq 1
, we abbreviate
L^{p}_{M}:=L^{p}(M,\mu)
. For functions
f,g:M\to\mathbb{R}
, we
write
f\lesssim g
(equivalently
f(x)\lesssim g(x)
uniformly in
x\in M
)
if there is a constant
C>0
such that
f(x)\leq Cg(x)
for every
x\in M
,
and
f\asymp g
if
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f
. When used repeatedly the
implicit constants may change from line to line. For
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
t\wedge s:=\min\{t,s\}
and
t\vee s:=\max\{t,s\}
. Finally we set

\mathbb{Z}_{+}:=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
and
\mathbb{N}:=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
.

For (
1.1
) to formally make sense, we assume that
L
is a
non-negative, densely defined, self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}_{M}
with domain

\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)
, and that
L
generates a Markov semigroup

(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, written interchangeably as
e^{-tL}=P_{t}
. We further assume
that
P_{t}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu
for every
t>0
,
so that there exists a symmetric, everywhere-defined continuous function

p_{t}:M\times M\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying

P_{t}f(x)=\int_{M}p_{t}(x,y)f(y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y)

for every non-negative measurable
f
and every
x\in M
for which the
integral converges. We call
p_{t}(x,y)
the
heat
kernel
of
L
.

By the classical theory of Dirichlet
forms (See
[
52
, Theorem 1.3.1]
, also see
[
35
,
87
]
),
L
is
associated with a symmetric quadratic form: for
f,g\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(f,g):=\int_{M}f(Lg)\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

(1.2)

whose domain
\mathcal{F}\supset\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)
makes
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
a Dirichlet form on
L^{2}_{M}
. More details will be discussed in Section
2
.

We assume the following for the rest of the paper.

Assumption 1.1
.

With
(M,d,\mu)
,
L
,
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(p_{t}(x,y),\ t>0,\ x,y\in M)
as
above, there exist constants
d_{h}\geq d_{w}\geq 2
and
\Theta\in(0,1]
such that
the following hold.

•

Sub-Gaussian heat-kernel bounds.
Set

\Phi(u):=\exp\!\bigl(-u^{d_{w}/(d_{w}-1)}\bigr)
for
u\in[0,\infty)
. There
are
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, uniformly in
(t,x,y)\in[0,1]\times M^{2}
,

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\,\Phi\!\left(C_{1}\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)\;\lesssim\;p_{t}(x,y)\;\lesssim\;t^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\,\Phi\!\left(C_{2}\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right).

(
\mathrm{HKE_{f}}
)

•

Spatial Hölder regularity.
Uniformly in
t\in(0,1]
and

x,x^{\prime},y\in M
with
d(x,x^{\prime})\leq t^{1/d_{w}}
,

\displaystyle\bigl|p_{t}(x,y)-p_{t}(x^{\prime},y)\bigr|\;\lesssim\;\left(\frac{d(x,x^{\prime})}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\!\Theta}t^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\,\Phi\!\left(C\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right).

(
\mathrm{HC}_{\Theta}
)

•

Stochastic completeness.
For every
t>0
and
x\in M
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y)=1.

(SC)

Examples and implications of Assumption
1.1
are numerous
and are deferred to Section
1.2
.

Assumption
1.1
implies the
chain condition
on the metric
space
(M,d)
(see
[
93
]
); as a result any
\alpha
-Hölder
continuous function with
\alpha>1
must be constant. By
[
55
, Theorem
4.1]
, it also entails volume regularity: uniformly in

x\in M
and
r\in(0,\mathrm{diam}(M)]
,

\displaystyle\mu(B(x,r))\asymp r^{d_{h}}.

(
V_{h}
)

In particular
d_{h}
is the Hausdorff dimension of
(M,d,\mu)
. Moreover, the
semigroup
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies the Feller and strong Feller properties
(see
[
85
]
): it is strongly continuous on
\mathcal{C}_{M}
,
and
P_{t}f\in\mathcal{C}_{M}
for every
t>0
and every bounded Borel measurable

f
on
M
.

Combining (

V
h

) with Lemma
2.2
, the heat-kernel estimates (

HKE
f

) can be recast in the
more compact volume-based form: there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, uniformly
in
x,y\in M
and
t>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu\!\left(B(x,t^{1/d_{w}})\right)}\Phi\!\left(C_{1}\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)\;\lesssim\;p_{t}(x,y)\;\lesssim\;\frac{1}{\mu\!\left(B(x,t^{1/d_{w}})\right)}\Phi\!\left(C_{2}\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right).

(1.3)

We now turn to the function spaces used to state our main results. Write

\norm{\cdot}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}}
for the supremum norm on
\mathcal{C}_{M}
and let
\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(A)
be the domain of an operator
A
in
\mathcal{C}_{M}
.

Definition 1.2
.

The space of
test functions
is

\mathcal{S}:=\bigl\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{M}:\ f\in\mathcal{D}_{\infty}(L^{n})\ \text{for every }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\bigr\}.

The space of
distributions

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is the topological dual of
\mathcal{S}
.

The semigroup-based building blocks of our Littlewood–Paley
decomposition are

\displaystyle Q^{(k)}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\left(tL\right)^{k}e^{-tL},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

(1.4)

\displaystyle P_{t}^{(k)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{m!}\,L^{m}e^{-Lt},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

We now introduce the Besov spaces that will serve as solution
spaces for (
1.1
) and the natural setting for
paraproducts. Our definition follows
[
86
]
(see
also
[
56
]
).

Definition 1.3
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,q\in(0,\infty]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with
k>\alpha/d_{w}
, set

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}:=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\!\left[t^{-\alpha/d_{w}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right]^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\!1/q},\qquad\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}:=\norm{P_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,q}^{\alpha}},

(1.5)

for
f\in\mathcal{S}
, with the usual modification when
q=\infty
. The
Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
is the closure of
\mathcal{S}
under

\norm{\cdot}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}
. When
q=\infty
we abbreviate

\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p}:=\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,

p\in[1,\infty]
.

The norms in (
1.5
) are equivalent for all
sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
; see
[
27
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Let
\dot{\xi}
be space–time white noise on
\mathbb{R}\times M
. Motivated by
[
39
]
, we decompose (
1.1
) into the stochastic linear part

\displaystyle\partial_{t}Y=-LY-Y+\dot{\xi},\quad\text{on }\mathbb{R}_{+}\times M

(1.6)

and the smoother remainder equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v=-Lv-v+\Psi(Y,v)\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{+}\times M,

(1.7)

with
\Psi(Y,v):=-\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}v^{j}Y^{:n-j:}
, where
Y^{:k:}
is the
k
-Wick power of
Y
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
(see Section
5
for more details on the re-normalizing counter terms).

Given the tuple
(d_{h},d_{w},\Theta,n)
as in Assumption
1.1
, we say equation (
1.1
) is in the
Da Prato-Debussche (DPD)
regime if for each
\phi_{0}
in the natural Besov space of
Y
in (
1.6
) (see Theorem
5.5
), there exist appropriate
(Y_{0},v_{0})
with
Y_{0}+v_{0}=\phi_{0}
, so that (
1.6
) has a solution global in
t\geq 0
and (
1.7
) has a local (in time) solution with initial conditions
Y_{0}
and
v_{0}
respectively.

Our main result gives a sufficient condition for the DPD regime with the convention that
a/0=\infty
for
a>0
. For the precise statement, see Lemma
6.2
and Corollary
6.10
.

Theorem 1.4
.

The tuple
(d_{h},d_{w},\Theta,n)
is in the DPD regime when

\displaystyle n<\frac{1}{2}\frac{d_{h}+d_{w}}{d_{h}-d_{w}},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad n<\frac{2\Theta}{d_{h}-d_{w}}+1.

(1.8)

If furthermore
n\geq 3
is odd, and

\displaystyle n<\frac{d_{w}}{d_{h}-d_{w}}.

(1.9)

Then for appropriate initial condition, the remainder equation (
1.7
) admits a function-valued global in time solution, and (
1.1
) defines a time-homogeneous Markov process on
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
, for any
\alpha>\frac{d_{h}-d_{w}}{2}
. In addition, it admits at least one invariant probability measure.

Remark 1.5
.

The first inequality in (
1.8
) comes from the Wick power of
Y
defined as a function of time rather than space-time distribution. The second inequality in (
1.8
) is due to the restriction for product estimates (see Theorem
1.7
). Inequality (
1.9
) is due to the need to control the Besov norm of the solution of (
1.7
) in terms of the Dirichlet energy (see Proposition
6.9
and Proposition
3.18
). This should be understood as the full regime of the energy argument of
[
91
,
109
]
.

Corollary 1.6
.

The
\Phi^{4}
equation is in the DPD regime whenever

\displaystyle\frac{d_{h}+d_{w}}{d_{h}-d_{w}}>6,\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{\Theta}{d_{h}-d_{w}}>1.

The appearance of the Hölder regularity
\Theta
of heat kernel in (
1.8
) is due to the following product estimates. See Section
4
for details.

Theorem 1.7

(Multiplicative inequality)
.

There exists a decomposition of product
f\cdot g
that agrees with the point-wise multiplication when
f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{M}
, so that

\alpha>0
,
\beta\in\left(-\Theta,\Theta\right)\backslash\{0\}
and
\alpha+\beta>0

\displaystyle\norm{f\cdot g}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha\wedge\beta}_{\infty}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}\cdot\norm{g}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}},

uniformly in
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty},\,g\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}

Remark 1.8

(Spectral dimension and admissible parameters)
.

The first condition in Theorem
1.4
admits a reformulation purely in terms of the spectral dimension

d_{s}=\frac{2d_{h}}{d_{w}}.

A direct computation yields

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d_{h}+d_{w}}{d_{h}-d_{w}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d_{s}+2}{d_{s}-2},

so that the corresponding restriction on
n
depends only on
d_{s}
. This is consistent with physics heuristics, where the spectral dimension governs the divergence of the linear stochastic convolution and therefore determines the renormalizability threshold.

In contrast, the second condition in Theorem
1.4
,

n<\frac{2\Theta}{d_{h}-d_{w}}+1,

cannot, in general, be expressed solely in terms of the spectral dimension, as it depends explicitly on the Hölder regularity exponent
\Theta
of the heat kernel. This shows that the Da Prato–Debussche regime is not determined purely by spectral dimension, but also by finer regularity properties of the underlying space.

Such a phenomenon does not arise in locally Euclidean Laplacian or fractional-Laplacian settings. For variable coefficient operators, similar in spirit restrictions have appeared, particularly in connection with homogenisation(see
[
34
, Definition 2.1, Proposition 2.9]
, see also
[
106
,
66
]
). However, we will see that the restriction in this setting is an effect of the rough local geometry.

1.2
Interpretations and examples

Scope of the assumption.

Assumption
1.1
is standard in the analysis of Dirichlet spaces and
covers many familiar examples—tori, compact Riemannian manifolds, metric
graphs, and a large class of fractals. Recall that
d_{w}
is the walk
dimension, which encodes the space–time scaling of the associated diffusion,
while
\Theta
quantifies the spatial Hölder regularity of the heat kernel.
The class of admissible geometries is in fact quite broad: for every pair

(d_{h},d_{w})
satisfying

2\leq d_{w}\leq 1+d_{h},

there exists a compact geodesic metric measure space, equipped with a
Dirichlet form, whose heat kernel obeys the sub-Gaussian
estimate (

HKE
f

) (c.f.
[
94
]
,
[
95
]
,
[
13
]
).

Spectral dimension and the singular regime.

A basic consequence of (

HKE
f

) is the short-time on-diagonal estimate

p_{t}(x,x)\asymp t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}},

which connects naturally to the spectral dimension

d_{s}:=\frac{2d_{h}}{d_{w}}.

The spectral dimension governs the regularity of the solution of (
1.6
): by the classic theory of Walsh
[
111
]
, a random field solution of (
1.6
) must satisfy

\mathbb{E}[Y(t,x)^{2}]=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-s}p_{s}(x,x)\,ds<\infty\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad d_{s}<2.

The value
d_{s}=2
thus marks the threshold between the random field and the singular regimes, and the assumption
d_{h}\geq d_{w}
is equivalent to the singular regime
d_{s}\geq 2
, where renormalization is unavoidable. In the case
d_{s}<2
, the conclusions of the main results will hold except that (
1.1
) admits a random field solution in the sense of Walsh
[
111
]
and has no renormalization conditions, and it would almost surely belong to a space of positive Besov spatial regularity.

Spatial regularity heuristics.

One notable aspect of Theorem
1.4
is that in addition to the dimensional quantities
d_{h}
and
d_{w}
, the maximal spatial Hölder regularity
\Theta
of the heat kernel must also be considered. As noted before, this is due to the appearance of
\Theta
in Theorem
1.7
, but notably, this is absent in the Fourier analytic formulation of the paraproduct decomposition in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(c.f.
[
9
, Chapter 2]
, see also
[
15
, Proposition 3.11]
). Also worth pointint out is that,
[
10
, Proposition 3.3]
has a similar restriction, but with
\Theta=1
(which they had assumed). There is evidence that this restriction is not an accident. First, unlike
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
or
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, our test function space
\mathcal{S}
needs not be an algebra: in fact, we know it cannot be if
d_{w}>2
[
21
]
. Thus, the way of defining arbitrary products of distributions with any test function fails in this setting. However, Hölder spaces are algebras, and it would make sense that we can define products of the Hölder space elements and spaces contained in their dual spaces. The fact that our Besov spaces only agree with Hölder spaces up to
\Theta
(Proposition
3.7
) is also reminiscent of recent triviality results for other notions of Besov spaces
[
18
,
82
]
.

The parameters not entirely independent: by
[
12
, Proof of Lemma
4.6]
and
[
6
, Lemma 3.4]
, the
sub-Gaussian bound (

HKE
f

) already implies (

HC
Θ

)
for
some
exponent
\Theta\in(0,1]
. What is generally
not
known
is the optimal value of
\Theta
. This becomes decisive for identifying concrete Da Prato–Debussche regimes. For this reason,
although the lower bound in (

HKE
f

) is not essential for most of our
analytic arguments, we retain the full two-sided estimate in our assumption. In addition, it keeps the link with the spectral dimension transparent, and for many examples the necessary and sufficient conditions for such two-sided bounds are now well understood.

Quantitative estimates for
\Theta
.

In certain cases
\Theta
can be controlled explicitly in terms of

(d_{h},d_{w})
. It is known that spaces with heat kernels satisfying (

HKE
f

) but with
d_{h}<d_{w}
, we have

\Theta\geq d_{w}-d_{h}\qquad\text{whenever }1\leq d_{h}<d_{w}\leq d_{h}+1,

with equality (in other words, optimality) if
M
is a post-critically finite self-similar
fractals
[
14
,
6
]
. In the complementary
regime
d_{w}\leq d_{h}
, no general lower bound on
\Theta
is available. This is why the second inequality in Theorem
1.4
is
substantive: even when the spectral-dimension restriction is favourable,
insufficient heat-kernel regularity may still rule out the Da
Prato–Debussche argument.

A classical benchmark.

Suppose that the heat kernel satisfies two-sided Gaussian bounds, i.e.
d_{w}=2
, and is spatially Lipschitz, i.e.
\Theta=1
. Corollary
1.6
then places
\Phi^{4}
in the Da Prato–Debussche regime if

d_{h}<\frac{14}{5},

in agreement with the fractional-dimensional heuristic
of
[
26
, Section 2.8.2]
. Whether one can actually
realize

d_{w}=2
,
\Theta=1
, and an arbitrary Hausdorff dimension
d_{h}

simultaneously appears to be open:
Laakso’s construction
[
83
]
yields spaces with arbitrary
d_{h}
and
d_{w}=2
, but no quantitative control of
\Theta
is known there.

Construction of Examples.

Figure
1
presents two complementary views of
Corollary
1.6
; the two panels should be read differently.

The
left
panel is built from a product-space construction. Starting
from a space
X
for which
\Theta\geq d_{w}-d_{h}
,
d_{h}(X)<d_{w}(X)
, one considers the product

M=X\times X.

Under this operation the walk dimension is preserved while the Hausdorff
dimension doubles,

d_{w}(M)=d_{w}(X),\qquad d_{h}(M)=2d_{h}(X),

and the Hölder exponent descends from the single factor
[
6
, Theorems 3.7 and 3.8]
. The left panel
therefore records precisely those product constructions
M=X\times X
for
which the minimal Hölder regularity possible still allows
M
to be in the
\Phi^{4}
Da Prato–Debussche regime; it is
not

a plot for arbitrary metric measure spaces
M
. In this picture the Vicsek
product lies inside the admissible region, while the product of two
Sierpiński gaskets lies outside. Notably, for spaces
M=X\times X
, the second inequality in (
1.8
) coincides with (
1.9
). In other words, they
also all admit global solutions (and thus invariant measures). There may be a deeper reason behind this, but we shall not explore it further in this project.

The
right
panel considers a different scenaio. If one sets aside the
difficulty that
\Theta
is typically unknown and simply imposes the
benchmark assumption
\Theta=1
, then Corollary
1.6
reduces,
for
\Phi^{4}
, to

d_{h}<\tfrac{7}{5}\,d_{w},\qquad d_{h}<d_{w}+1.

The right panel is therefore a benchmark picture: the maximal region
predicted by our criterion under the strongest realistic assumption on the
spatial regularity of the heat kernel.

Figure 1:
Admissible parameter regimes in the
(d_{w},d_{h})
-plane. Left: the product construction
M=X^{2}
, starting from a one-factor Barlow fractals
X
with
d_{h}(X)<d_{w}(X)
and quantitatively known heat-kernel Hölder exponent. The pale-blue background is the Barlow fractal range
2\leq d_{w}\leq d_{h}+1
, while the green region is the singular
\Phi^{4}
global-solution regime on
M=X^{2}
. Right: the benchmark case
\Theta=1
, corresponding to Lipschitz spatial regularity of the heat kernel. The green region is the global-solution regime, whereas the yellow region satisfies the local theory but lies outside the global condition.

Remark 1.9
.

The number
\frac{8}{3}
appearing in both figures should have a deeper meaning, as it is known that in the Gaussian case
(d_{w}=2)
, the invariant measure of the
\Phi^{4}_{d_{h}}
equation(interpreted using
(-\Delta)^{-\alpha}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
) is absolutely continuous with respect to the Gaussian free field for
d_{h}<\frac{8}{3}

[
62
, Corollary 4.5]
. We conjecture that adapting the content of
[
62
]
, one can show that the invariant measures of (
1.1
) constructed in this paper are all absolutely continuous with respect to the Gaussian free field, defined in (
5.5
).

Two fractal examples.

We list two well-known examples from the above to show that the
\Theta
-constraint is relevant: it can separate solvable from non-solvable regimes even when the scaling condition alone points in the same direction.

Example 1.10

(A product space in the
\Phi^{4}
Da Prato–Debussche regime)
.

Let
V
be the finite Vicsek fractal. By
[
6
, Theorem
3.7]
,
V
satisfies Assumption
1.1
with

d_{h}^{\prime}=\frac{\ln 5}{\ln 3},\qquad d_{w}=\frac{\ln 5}{\ln 3}+1,\qquad\Theta=1.

Equip
M:=V\times V
with the product metric and product measure. Then
Assumption
1.1
holds on
M
with the same
d_{w}
and
\Theta
and
with
d_{h}=2d_{h}^{\prime}
. A direct computation gives

\frac{d_{h}+d_{w}}{d_{h}-d_{w}}\approx 11.6>6,\qquad\frac{\Theta}{d_{h}-d_{w}}\approx 2.15>2,

so Corollary
1.6
applies:
\Phi^{4}
on
M
lies in the Da
Prato–Debussche regime.

Example 1.11

(A space excluded by the
\Theta
-condition)
.

Let
M=T\times T
, where
T
is the Sierpiński gasket. Again
by
[
6
, Theorem 3.7]
,

d_{h}=2\,\frac{\ln 3}{\ln 2}\approx 3.170,\qquad d_{w}=\frac{\ln 5}{\ln 2}\approx 2.322,\qquad\Theta=d_{w}-\frac{d_{h}}{2}\approx 0.737.

Then

\frac{d_{h}+d_{w}}{d_{h}-d_{w}}\approx\frac{5.492}{0.848}>6,\qquad\frac{\Theta}{d_{h}-d_{w}}\approx 0.87<1.

The scaling condition is satisfied, but the Hölder-regularity condition
fails, and
\Phi^{4}
on
M
is not covered by Corollary
1.6
.

The one-factor spaces underlying the two examples are depicted in Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
Left: the Sierpiński gasket. Right: the Vicsek fractal.
These are the one-factor spaces used in the product examples above.

Future directions.

Below are some interesting potential future directions motivated by the existing literature and the current work.

1.

Here, we do not obtain the uniqueness of the invariant measure. Uniqueness typically requires additional mixing properties (for instance irreducibility and an
(asymptotic) strong Feller
property), which are not proved here. We conjecture that one should be able to use the argument of
[
109
]
to produce a spectral gap for
\mathbf{P}_{t}
, the semigroup on probability measures on the relevant Besov space defined by the solution. We also conjecture that the argument of
[
33
]
or
[
67
]
can be adapted to show that the unique invariant measure is non-Gaussian.

2.

The discrepancy between the local and global solution regimes in our setting is possibly due to the lack of sharpness of the energy method we adapted. More precisely, a careful examination of Section
6.2
reveals that this gap is due to the need to control a higher regularity Besov norm than provided by the local solution condition (except in the case
d_{h}=d_{w}
, which generalizes
\mathbb{T}^{2}
exactly). It is possible that a more optimal exploitation of the coercive nature of the backward superlinear forcing will close this gap, as has been done in Euclidean settings for
\Phi^{4}

[
92
,
58
,
36
]
. This idea could also be explored in combination with paracontrolled calculus.

3.

It is also natural to wonder if the machinery we built up here can be extended to other singular SPDEs. For instance,
[
10
]
originally addressed some parabolic Anderson models, and
[
78
]
has introduced a inverse Cole-Hopf transform which removed the need to use paracontrolled calculus for the
\Phi^{4}
equation. The latter strategy has been generalized to Hermitian vector bundles on 3-manifolds in
[
11
]
. It is also known that the dynamical sine-Gordon equation has a Da Prato-Debussche regime on
\mathbb{T}^{2}

[
64
,
57
]
, and one may expect to find the same in the analogous generalization in our setting:
d_{h}=d_{w}
. However, the literature of all of these equations indicate that the analytic difficulties we encounter here manifest as different problems for each equation.

4.

More intricate sub-Gaussian heat-kernel estimates arise on more irregular geometries, such as continuum random trees
[
38
]
and Liouville quantum gravity surfaces
[
88
,
7
,
44
]
. Given the interest of quantum field theory in some of these spaces
[
43
]
, deriving an equation of the form (
1.1
) and extending the results of the present paper to this setting would constitute a genuinely novel non-perturbative approach in these settings.

1.3
Paper Organization

We first recollect some basic Dirichlet form preliminaries in Section
2
and prove some elementary functional inequalities involving the heat-kernel based Besov norms in Section
3
.
In Section
4
, we construct the paraproduct and prove
Theorem
1.7
and a Schauder-type estimate (Proposition
4.10
) using the strategy of
[
10
]
. In section
5
, we construct Wick powers of
Y
and compute their regularities. Then in section
6
, we will develop local and global solutions for
v
using the methods of
[
39
,
91
,
109
]
. Finally, we employ the machinery developed in
[
33
]
to view the solution
\phi
as a time-homogeneous Markov process on a proper state space
\mathfrak{C}
, to which we can apply the Krylov-Bogliubov method and obtain an invariant measure.

2
Preliminaries

This section collects the basic facts about Dirichlet forms, the associated semigroup and generator, and the energy measure that will be used freely throughout the paper, and then records several preliminary estimates. The material is standard but scattered across the literature; for the convenience of the reader, proofs that are not conveniently available in the literature are collected in Appendix
8
. We claim no novelty for the results in this section. For a systematic treatment of Dirichlet forms we refer to
[
52
,
35
,
87
]
, and for the general semigroup theory to
[
114
]
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
be a densely defined, closed, non-negative definite symmetric bilinear form on
L^{2}_{M}
, where
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
is a Hilbert space under the inner product

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}(u,v):=\mathcal{E}(u,v)+\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}_{M}}.

A map
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
normal contraction
if
\phi(0)=0
and
\absolutevalue{\phi(s) - \phi(t)}\leq\absolutevalue{s-t}
for all
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
. The form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
is called a
Dirichlet form
if it is
Markovian
, i.e.,
\phi\circ u\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{E}(\phi\circ u,\phi\circ u)\leq\mathcal{E}(u,u)
for every
u\in\mathcal{F}
and every normal contraction
\phi
; see
[
52
, Theorem 1.4.1]
.

A Dirichlet form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
is called
regular
if
\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{C}_{M}
is simultaneously dense in
\mathcal{C}_{M}
in the supremum norm and dense in
\mathcal{F}
in the
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{1/2}
-norm; any such dense subalgebra is called a
core
of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
. It is called
strongly local
if
\mathcal{E}(u,v)=0
whenever
u,v\in\mathcal{F}
and
u
is
\mu
-a.e. constant on an open neighborhood of the support of
v
. Equivalently, the Beurling–Deny decomposition of
\mathcal{E}
contains neither jump nor killing part
[
52
, Theorem 3.2.1]
. Under Assumption
1.1
, the Dirichlet form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
associated with
L
is both regular and strongly local; see
[
17
]
.

Every regular Dirichlet form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
determines a unique non-negative definite self-adjoint operator
L
on
L^{2}_{M}
with domain
\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)\subset\mathcal{F}
, characterized by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\langle Lu,v\rangle_{L^{2}_{M}},\qquad u\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L),\ v\in\mathcal{F},

(2.1)

and
-L
generates a strongly continuous Markovian semigroup
\set{P_{t}}_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}_{M}
(see
[
52
, Theorem 1.3.1]
and
[
114
, Chapter IX]
).

For every
u,v\in\mathcal{F}\cap L^{\infty}_{M}
, there exists a unique finite signed Radon measure
\Gamma(u,v)
on
M
, called the
energy measure
of
u
and
v
, determined by the identity

\displaystyle\int_{M}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{E}(\varphi u,v)+\mathcal{E}(\varphi v,u)-\mathcal{E}(\varphi,uv)\right),\qquad\varphi\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{C}_{M}.

(2.2)

The construction extends by truncation to arbitrary
u,v\in\mathcal{F}
; see
[
52
, Section 3.2]
. By a slight abuse of notation, for
u,v\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)\cap L^{\infty}_{M}
we also write

\Gamma(u,v)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(uLv+vLu-L(uv)\right),

(2.3)

which is to be read as the identity (
2.2
) upon integration against a test function in
\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{C}_{M}
. By
[
52
, Lemma 5.4.3]
, we have the following Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for energy measures: for
f,g\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{C}_{M}
and
u,v\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\absolutevalue{fg}\mathrm{d}\absolutevalue{\Gamma\pr{u,v}}\leq\left(\int_{M}f^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(u,u)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{M}g^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(v,v)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.4)

The form
\mathcal{E}
and its energy measure are related by
\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(u,v)
whenever
1\in\mathcal{F}
, which holds in particular under Assumption
1.1
since
M
is compact. Under strong locality,
\Gamma
satisfies the Leibniz rule

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Gamma(uv,w)=u\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(v,w)+v\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(u,w),\qquad u,v,w\in\mathcal{F}\cap L^{\infty}_{M},

and the chain rule
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(g(u),v)=g^{\prime}(u)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(u,v)
for any
g\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with bounded derivative; see
[
52
, Theorem 3.2.2]
or
[
35
, Chapter 4]
. Strong locality and the Leibniz rule will be used repeatedly, and will be essential to the construction of a global solution of (
1.1
).

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
a>0
and set
c_{a}:=\inf_{x,y\in M}p_{a}(x,y)\in(0,1)
. Suppose
p\in[1,\infty]
and
g\in L^{p}_{M}
with
\int_{M}g\mathrm{d}\mu=0
, then

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-tL}g}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq(1-c_{a})^{\left\lfloor\frac{t}{a}\right\rfloor}\norm{g}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

Under two-sided heat kernel estimates, (

HC
Θ

) can be strengthened.

Lemma 2.2
.

Uniformly in
t\geq 1
and
x,y\in M
,
p_{t}(x,y)\asymp 1
. In addition, there exists
c,C>0
, the following inequality holds uniformly in
t>0
and
x,x^{\prime},y\in M
with
d(x,x^{\prime})<t^{1/d_{w}}

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{p_t(x,y) - p_t(x',y)}\lesssim\left(\frac{d(x,x^{\prime})}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\Theta}t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}}\exp\left(-C\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{w}}{d_{w}-1}}-ct\right).

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t>0
,
Q^{(k)}_{t}
admits a symmetric density function
q_{k,t}
w.r.t
\mu
, so that

\displaystyle t^{k}\partial_{t}^{k}p_{t}(x,y)=q_{k,t}(x,y),\qquad\int_{M}q_{k,t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)=0,\qquad(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times M^{2}.

(2.5)

There exists
C,c>0
so that

•

uniformly in
t>0
and
x,y\in M
,

\displaystyle|q_{k,t}(x,y)|\lesssim t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}}\exp\left(-C\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{w}}{d_{w}-1}}-ct\right).

(2.6)

•

uniformly in
t>0
and
x,x^{\prime},y\in M
with
d(x,x^{\prime})\leq t^{1/d_{w}}
,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{q_{k,t}(x,y) - q_{k,t}(x',y)}\lesssim\left(\frac{d(x,x^{\prime})}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\Theta}t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}}\exp\left(-C\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{w}}{d_{w}-1}}-ct\right).

(2.7)

Consequently, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, it holds uniformly in
t>0
and
f\in L^{p}_{M}
that

\displaystyle\norm{Q^{(k)}_tf}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim e^{-ct}\norm{f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

Lemma 2.4
.

[
6
, Lemma 2.3]

For any
\alpha\in[0,\infty)
, there exists
c=c(\alpha)>0
so that uniformly in
t>0
and
x,y\in M
,

d(x,y)^{\alpha}p_{t}(x,y)\lesssim\left(t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\wedge 1\right)p_{ct}(x,y).

Lemma 2.5
.

For
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
, uniformly in
t>0
,

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-tL}f}_{L^{q}_{M}}\lesssim\left(1+t^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)}\right)\norm{f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

3
Heat semi-group based Besov Spaces

In this section, we explore heat semi-group based Besov spaces and their relevant properties. We will first list some elementary properties of Besov norms. Next, we study how they relate to Hölder spaces and with each other via duality, interpolation, various embedding properties, and the smoothing effect of heat flow. We will also study how Besov norms are related to Dirichlet forms and state some useful inequalities that will help us analyze
L^{p}_{M}
-norms of solutions to nonlinear (S)PDEs. We define
\alpha
-Hölder norm
\norm{\cdot}_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{M}}
for
\alpha\in(0,1]
:

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{M}}:=\norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}}+\sup_{0<d(x,y)\leq 1}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{d(x,y)^{\alpha}}.

3.1
Elementary properties of Besov norms

The following almost orthogonality relation will be useful: for each
p\in[1,\infty]
,
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
and
t,s>0
,

\displaystyle\norm{Q^{(k_1)}_sQ^{(k_2)}_t}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\norm{e^{-t\wedge sL } \min\left\{ \pr{\frac{t}{s}}^{k_2}, \pr{\frac{s}{t}}^{k_1}\right\} Q^{(k_1+k_2)}_{t\vee s}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{p}_{M}}

(3.1)

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{k_{2}},\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{k_{1}}\right\}\norm{ Q^{(k_1+k_2)}_{t\vee s}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{p}_{M}}.

We will need the following version of Calderon’s reproducing formula.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
f\in L^{p}_{M}
, then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\lim_{t\downarrow 0}P_{t}^{(k)}f=f,\quad\text{ in }L^{p}_{M}

and

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{\gamma_{k}}\int_{0}^{1}Q_{t}^{(k)}f\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}+P^{(k)}_{1}(f),\qquad\text{in }L^{p}_{M}.

If
f\in\mathcal{C}_{M}
, then the convergence is in
\mathcal{C}_{M}
.

The proof of the case for
f\in L^{p}_{M}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
can be found in
[
56
, Proposition 2.7]
. For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{M}
, this proof applies
mutatis mutandis
by Feller property of
(P_{t})_{t}
.

The Besov norms defined in (
1.5
) are independent of the index
k
for sufficiently large
k
. This is essentially shown in
[
56
, Proposition 2.9]
, and we will omit its proof.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, the norm
\norm{\cdot}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}
defined as in Definition
1.3
is equivalent for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with
k>\alpha
.

Proposition 3.3
.

For
\sigma\in(-\infty,d_{w})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{P^{(k)}_1f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

Proof.

[
10
, Proposition 2.7.]
is this proposition with
d_{w}=2
. The proof there can be adapted
mutatis mutandis
, so we omit it.
∎

The following proposition shows that the Besov norm is also independent of the time horizon.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, then for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with
k>\alpha
and
T\in[1,\infty]
, denote for
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
that

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}(T)}:=\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}}+\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left(t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

with obvious modifications when
q=\infty
. Then for any
T,T^{\prime}\in[1,\infty]
, it holds uniformly in
f\in\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}
that

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}(T)}\asymp\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}(T^{\prime})}

Proof.

Note that it is enough to take
T=1
and
T^{\prime}=\infty
and prove the
\gtrsim
direction. Observe that by stochastic completeness and Proposition
3.3
, we have for any
k>0
and
s>0

\int_{M}L^{k}e^{-sL}f(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=0.

Thus by Lemma
2.1
, we have for
t\in[1,2]
,

\displaystyle\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\norm{e^{-(t - 1)L} (tL)^kP_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq t^{k}\norm{L^k P_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}},\qquad t\in[1,2],

\displaystyle\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\norm{e^{-(t - 1)L} (tL)^kP_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq t^{k}(1-c_{1})^{\left\lfloor t-1\right\rfloor}\norm{L^k P_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}},\qquad t\in[2,\infty),

where
c_{1}=\inf_{x,y}p_{1}(x,y)\in(0,1)
by the lower bound in (

HKE
f

). Therefore, for any
p\in[1,\infty]
and
q\in[1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{1}^{\infty}\left(t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\norm{L^k P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}},

and
\sup_{t\geq 1}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_t^{(k)} f }_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{L^k P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.
When
q=\infty
, we see

\displaystyle\sup_{t>0}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_t^{(k)} f }_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_t^{(k)} f }_{L^{p}_{M}}+\norm{L^k P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_t^{(k)} f }_{L^{p}_{M}}.

For
q<\infty
, observe that uniformly in
s\in[1/2,1],

\displaystyle\norm{L^k P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}=s^{-k}\norm{e^{-(1-s)L}(sL)^k e^{-sL}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{Q^{(k)}_s f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

Hence

\displaystyle\norm{L^k P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\int_{1/2}^{1}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{1/2}^{1}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_s f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

which implies the desired inequality.
∎

3.2
Regularizing effect of heat flow

The following results demonstrates the regularization effects of the heat semi-group.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{p,\infty}
for some
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.
Let
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\sigma\leq\alpha
, then for
a\in\mathbb{N}
with
a\geq\alpha/d_{w}
and
s>0
that

\displaystyle\norm{Q_s^{(b)} f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}}\leq s^{\frac{\sigma-\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{\sigma}_{p,\infty}}.

Furthermore, if
\sigma\leq 0
, then uniformly in
t\in(0,1]

\displaystyle\norm{P_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\begin{cases}t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{\sigma}_{p,\infty}}+\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}},&\text{if }\sigma<0,\\
\log(1/t)\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{0}_{p,\infty}}+\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}},&\text{if }\sigma=0,\end{cases}

In particular, for each
s>0
,
Q^{(b)}_{s}f\in\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}
for all
\alpha>0
.

Proof.

A simple calculation gives

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_t^{(a)} Q_s^{(b)}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:almost orthogonal}}{\leq}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{a}\wedge\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{b}\norm{Q^{(a+b)}_{t\vee s}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{a}\wedge\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{b}\left(t\vee s\right)^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,\infty}^{\sigma}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_t^{(a)} Q_s^{(b)}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq\begin{cases}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}+a}s^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}-a}\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,\infty}^{\sigma}},&t\leq s\\
t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}-b+\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}s^{b}\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,\infty}^{\sigma}},&t\geq s.\end{cases}

Since
a>\alpha/d_{w}
,
\sigma\leq\alpha
and
b\geq 0
, we see

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,s]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}+a}s^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}-a}=s^{\frac{\sigma-\alpha}{d_{w}}}=\sup_{t\geq s}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}-b+\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}s^{b},

which proves the first inequality. For the second inequality, we have by the reverse triangle inequality

\displaystyle\norm{P_tf}_{L^{p}_{M}}-\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq\norm{P_tf - P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq\int_{t}^{1}\norm{Q^{(1)}_sf}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\leq\int_{t}^{1}s^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,\infty}^{\sigma}},

from which the desired inequalities follow by single variable calculus.
∎

Next we show strong continuity of the heat semi-group acting on Besov spaces.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then uniformly in
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}
and
t\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\norm{P_t f - f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma-\delta}_{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

For any
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\norm{P_tf - f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\lambda}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{\infty}}.

As a consequence,
P_{t}
is strongly continuous on
\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}
for every
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
k\geq 0
be sufficiently large, and consider for
0<\tau<t
that

\displaystyle\tau^{-\frac{\sigma-\delta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{Q_\tau^{(k)} Q_s^{(k)} f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau^{-\frac{\sigma-\delta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{\min\{\tau,s\}}{\max\{\tau,s\}}\right)^{k}\norm{Q_{\max\{\tau,s\}}^{(2k)} f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\tau^{-\frac{\sigma-\delta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{\min\{\tau,s\}}{\max\{\tau,s\}}\right)^{k}\max\{\tau,s\}^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\,\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{\min\{\tau,s\}}{\max\{\tau,s\}}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{\max\{\tau,s\}}{\tau}\right)^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\,\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\left(\frac{s}{\tau}\right)^{k}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}+\int_{\tau}^{t}\left(\frac{\tau}{s}\right)^{k-\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\tau^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\left(\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1-\left(\frac{\tau}{t}\right)^{k-\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}}{\,k-\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}\,}\right)\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

If
\tau\geq t
, then

\displaystyle\tau^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{s}{\tau}\right)^{k}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}=\frac{1}{k}\tau^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\left(\frac{t}{\tau}\right)^{k}\leq\frac{1}{k}\,t^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}},

which implies

\displaystyle\tau^{-\frac{\sigma-\delta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{Q^{(k)}_\tau Q^{(k)}_s f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\lesssim t^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

By taking supremum in
\tau\in(0,1]
, we see by Theorem
3.1
that

\displaystyle\norm{P_tf -f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\sigma-\delta}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

Similarly, consider

\displaystyle\norm{P_1\pr{P_t f -f}}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\norm{Q^{(1)}_s P_1f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\norm{P_1f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}_{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\delta}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}},

where we used the fact that
\delta\in(0,1)
and Proposition
3.2
in the second inequality and Lemma
3.5
in the last inequality. Collecting the estimates above yields the first inequality.

Now consider for
\lambda>0
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{P_tf - f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\norm{Q^{(k)}_sf}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}s^{\frac{\lambda}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\lambda}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}_{\infty}}.

For the strong continuity of
P_{t}
, we let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}
and choose
\{f_{n}\}_{n}\subset\mathcal{B}^{1+\absolutevalue{\alpha}}_{\infty}
that converges to
f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}
. Then by Lemma
3.5
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\norm{P_tf - f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\norm{P_t(f-f_n)}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}+\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\norm{P_tf_n - f_n}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}+\norm{f_n - f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{f - f_n}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}.

Sending
n\to\infty
yields the desired result.
∎

3.3
Relation to Hölder space and dyadic formulation

The following proposition determines the relation between the Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}
and Hölder space
{\mathcal{C}}^{\sigma}_{M}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Recall
\Theta\in(0,1]
in (

HC
Θ

). The following inequalities holds uniformly for
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\asymp\norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}},\qquad\text{ if }\sigma\in(0,\Theta)

(3.2)

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}},\qquad\text{ if }\sigma\in(0,1).

(3.3)

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\sigma}
. By Proposition
2.3
, we see for any
t>0
,
a\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in M
,

\displaystyle Q^{(a)}_{t}f(x)=Q^{(a)}_{t}\left(f(\cdot)-f(x)\right)(x)=\int_{M}\left(q_{a,t}(x,y)\left(f(y)-f(x)\right)\right)\mu(\mathrm{d}y).

Therefore, by Lemma
2.4
and Proposition
2.3
, we see uniformly in
t\in(0,1]
and
x\in M
that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{Q^{(a)}_tf(x)}

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{M}q_{a,t}(x,y)(f(z)-f(x))\mu(dz)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\norm{f}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\sigma}}\int_{M}\left|q_{a,t}(x,y)\right|d(x,y)^{\sigma}\mu(dy)

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\sigma}},

which implies
\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\sigma}}.
This completes the proof of (
3.3
).

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}
, for some
0<\sigma<\Theta\leq 1
. Since
\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}<1
, by Calderon’s reproducing formula

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{P_1f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}+\int_{0}^{1}\norm{Q^{(1)}_tf}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}\left(1+\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

Moreover, for any two points
x,y\in M
with
0<d(x,y)\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle f(x)-f(y)

\displaystyle=P_{1}f(x)-P_{1}f(y)+Q^{(1)}_{1}f(x)-Q^{(1)}_{1}f(y)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{0}^{1}Q^{(2)}_{t}f(x)-Q^{(2)}_{t}f(y)\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

By (

HC
Θ

) and Proposition
2.3
, it holds uniformly in
x,y\in M
that

\displaystyle\left|P_{1}f(x)-P_{1}f(y)\right|+\absolutevalue{Q_1^{(1)} f(x) - Q^{(1)}_1f(y)}\lesssim d(x,y)^{\Theta}\norm{f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}.

Now observe that
Q^{(2)}_{t}=16Q^{(2)}_{\frac{t}{2}}Q^{(2)}_{\frac{t}{2}}
. So for
d(x,y)\leq t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}
, by Proposition
2.3
again,

\displaystyle\left|Q^{(2)}_{t}f(x)-Q^{(2)}_{t}f(y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{M}\left|q_{2,t/2}(x,z)-q_{2,t/2}(y,z)\right|\left|Q^{2}_{t/2}f(z)\right|\mu(dz)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}}\right)^{\Theta}\norm{Q^{(2)}_{t/2}f}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}}\right)^{\Theta}t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}

If
t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}\leq d(x,y)\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|Q^{(2)}_{t}f(x)-Q^{(2)}_{t}f(y)\right|\lesssim\norm{Q^{(2)}_tf}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{1}Q_{t}^{(2)}f(x)-Q^{(2)}_{t}f(y)\right|\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{0}^{d(x,y)^{d_{w}}}t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}+\int_{d(x,y)^{d_{w}}}^{1}\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}}\right)^{\Theta}t^{\frac{\sigma}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(d(x,y)^{\sigma}+d(x,y)^{\Theta}\right)\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

Therefore, since
d(x,y)\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(d(x,y)^{\sigma}+d(x,y)^{\Theta}\right)\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\lesssim d(x,y)^{\min\{\sigma,\Theta\}}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

So if
\sigma\leq\Theta
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\sigma}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty}}.

∎

Sometimes it is more convenient to work with a discrete version of the Besov norm, which resembles the Littlewood-Paley formulation. The following proposition gives such a version.

Proposition 3.8
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k>\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}
. Let
t_{j}:=2^{-d_{w}j}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,

A_{j}(f):=\left(t_{j}L\right)^{k}P_{t_{j}}f.

Then for any
p\in[1,\infty]
and
q\in[1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(2^{j\alpha}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}},

with the constant depending only on
\alpha
,
k
and
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
but not
f
. When
q=\infty
,

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}}\asymp\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}2^{j\alpha}\norm{A_j f}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

Proof.

Let
t\in(0,1]
, choose
j\in\mathbb{N}
so that
2^{-d_{w}(j+1)}<t\leq 2^{-d_{w}j}
, then

\displaystyle 2^{-\alpha}\leq t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}2^{-\alpha(j+1)}\leq 1\text{ for }\alpha\geq 0,\qquad 2^{-\alpha}\geq t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}2^{-\alpha(j+1)}\geq 1\text{ for }\alpha<0.

(3.4)

Hence,

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{ \pr{tL}^k P_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{t^{-\alpha/d_{w}}}{2^{\alpha d_{w}(j+1)/d_{w}}}\right)\left(\frac{t}{2^{-d_{w}(j+1)}}\right)^{k}2^{\alpha(j+1)}\norm{\pr{2^{-d_w{(j+1)}}L}^kP_{t-t_{j+1}}P_{t_{j+1}}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:dyadic dw decomposition of time}}{\lesssim}\norm{P_{t - t_{j+1}}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{p}_{M}}2^{\alpha(j+1)}\norm{\pr{2^{-d_w(j+1)}L}^kP_{t_{j+1}}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=2^{\alpha(j+1)}\norm{A_{j+1}(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle 2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=2^{\alpha j}\norm{\pr{t_j L}^k P_{t_j} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}2^{\alpha j}\left(t^{-1}2^{-jd_{w}}\right)^{k}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{P_{t_j - t}(tL)^k P_tf}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{P_{t_j - t}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{p}_{M}}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{(tL)^k P_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{(tL)^k P_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

Hence we have

\displaystyle 2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{(tL)^k P_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim 2^{\alpha(j+1)}\norm{A_{j+1} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

(3.5)

We now prove the inequality for
q\neq\infty
. For the upper bound, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left(t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{\pr{tL}^kP_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{2^{-d_{w}(j+1)}}^{2^{-d_{w}j}}\left(t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{\pr{tL}^kP_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq: comparible of time point and dyadic}}{\lesssim}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}2^{d_{w}j}\left(2^{-d_{w}j}-2^{-d_{w}(j+1)}\right)\left(2^{\alpha(j+1)}\norm{A_{j+1} (f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(2^{\alpha(j)}\norm{A_{j} (f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}.

For the lower bound, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left(t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{\pr{tL}^kP_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{2^{-d_{w}(j+1)}}^{2^{-d_{w}j}}\left(t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{\pr{tL}^kP_t f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq: comparible of time point and dyadic}}{\gtrsim}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}2^{d_{w}(j+1)}\left(2^{-d_{w}j}-2^{-d_{w}(j+1)}\right)\left(2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sum_{j\geq 0}\left(2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\norm{P_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\left(2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\left(\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(2^{\alpha(j)}\norm{A_{j} (f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

which implies the desired inequality.

The inequality for
q=\infty
follows by taking supremum over
j\in\mathbb{N}
in (
3.5
).∎

3.4
Duality and Interpolation Inequalities

Lemma 3.9

(Duality)
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}\in[1,+\infty]
satisfy
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
. Then for any smooth
f,g:M\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\absolutevalue{\braket{f,g}}:=\absolutevalue{\braket{f,g}_{L^2}}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\norm{g}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}}.

Hence, the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
extends to
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}\times\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}
continuously.

In light of Lemma
3.9
, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,\,q,\,p^{\prime},\,q^{\prime}\in[1,\infty]
satisfies the condition in Lemma
3.9
, we denote
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
as the pairing between elements in
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}
when there is no confusion going forward.

Proof.

Recall the Carderon’s reproducing formula from Theorem
3.1
, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k>\alpha
, we can write

\displaystyle\braket{f,g}

\displaystyle=\braket{f,P_{1}^{(2k)}g}+\int_{0}^{1}\braket{f,Q^{(2k)}_{t}g}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

By
[
56
, Lemma 3.3]
, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\braket{f,P_1^{(2k)}g}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{2k-1}p_{\ell}\absolutevalue{\braket{f, L^\ell P_1g}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{2k-1}\absolutevalue{\braket{e^{-\frac{1}{2}L}f, L^\ell e^{-\frac{1}{2}}g}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{2k-1}\norm{e^{-\frac{1}{2}L}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\norm{L^\ell e^{-\frac{1}{2}L}g}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{2k-1}\norm{e^{-\frac{1}{2}L}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\norm{L^\ell e^{-\frac{1}{4}L}}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{M}\to L^{p^{\prime}}_{M}}\norm{ e^{-\frac{1}{4}L}g}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\norm{g}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}}

For the seminorm part, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\absolutevalue{\int_0^1 \braket{f,Q_t^{(2k)}g} \frac{\mathrm dt}{t}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\absolutevalue{ \int_0^1\braket{f,t^{2k} L^{2k} e^{-tL} g} \frac{\mathrm dt}{t}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\absolutevalue{ \int_0^1\braket{(tL)^ke^{-\frac{t}{2}L}f,(tL)^k e^{-\frac{t}{2}L} g} \frac{\mathrm dt}{t}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{t/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{t/2} g}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{t/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{t/2} g}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{M}}\right)^{q^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\norm{g}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}}.

Using both estimates finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.10

(Interpolation)
.

Let
p,q\in[1,+\infty]
and
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. For
\eta\in(0,1)
, define
\alpha_{\eta}=\eta\alpha_{0}+(1-\eta)\alpha_{1}
. Then, we have

\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha_{\eta}}_{p_{\eta},q_{\eta}}}\leq\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha_{0}}_{p_{0},q_{0}}}^{\eta}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha_{1}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}^{1-\eta}

where
p_{0},p_{1},q_{0},q_{1}\in[1,+\infty]
satisfy

\frac{1}{p_{\eta}}=\frac{\eta}{p_{0}}+\frac{1-\eta}{p_{1}},\quad\frac{1}{q_{\eta}}=\frac{\eta}{q_{0}}+\frac{1-\eta}{q_{1}}.

Proof.

Let
k>0
be sufficiently large and let
z=1+\frac{(1-\eta)q_{0}}{\eta q_{1}}
, then
\eta zq_{\eta}=q_{0}
and
\frac{z}{z-1}(1-\eta)q_{\eta}=q_{1}
. By the
L^{p}
interpolation inequality and Hölder’s inequality, we have for any
\eta\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left[t^{-\frac{\alpha_{\eta}}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p_{\eta}}_{M}}\right]^{q_{\eta}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{\eta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left[t^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}\eta}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p_{0}}_{M}}^{\eta}t^{-\frac{\alpha_{1}(1-\eta)}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p_{1}}_{M}}^{1-\eta}\right]^{q_{\eta}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{\eta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}\eta zq_{\eta}}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_tf}_{L^{p_{0}}_{M}}^{\eta q_{\eta}z}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{zq_{\eta}}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\frac{(1-\eta)\alpha_{1}zq_{\eta}}{d_{w}(z-1)}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p_{1}}_{M}}^{(1-\eta)q_{\eta}\frac{z}{z-1}}\right)^{\frac{z-1}{zq_{\eta}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left[t^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tf}_{L^{p_{0}}_{M}}\right]^{q_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{\eta}{q_{0}}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left[t^{-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tf}_{L^{p_{1}}_{M}}\right]^{q_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1-\eta}{q_{1}}}.

This implies

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha_{\eta}}_{p_{\eta},q_{\eta}}}

\displaystyle=\norm{P^{(k)}_1 f}_{L^{p_{\eta}}_{M}}+\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left[t^{-\frac{\alpha_{\eta}}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_t f}_{L^{p_{\eta}}_{M}}\right]^{q_{\eta}}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q_{\eta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha_{0}}_{p_{0},q_{0}}}^{\eta}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha_{1}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}^{1-\eta}.

∎

3.5
Embedding Theorems

We now collect several standard and useful embedding properties of Besov spaces.

Proposition 3.11
.

[
86
, Section 4.2]

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,\,q\in(0,\infty]
, then

\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\alpha-d_{h}/p}_{\infty}.

In addition, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq p\leq q\leq+\infty
, the following inequality holds uniformly in
f\in\mathcal{B}_{p,p,}^{\alpha}
,

\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,p}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{q,q}}.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, then the following inequality holds uniformly for
f\in\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}.

Proof.

Suppose
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
so that
k>\alpha/d_{w}
. By Theorem
3.1
, we see

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tf}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tP^{(2k)}_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{1}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tQ^{(2k)}_s f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}.

We consider the first term on the right hand side. If
\alpha\leq 0
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tP^{(2k)}_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{P_{1/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

On the other hand, if
\alpha>0
, then by Lemma
3.5
that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tP^{(2k)}_1 f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{P_{1/2} f}_{\mathcal{B}^{0}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\norm{P_{1/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

For the second term, consider

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tQ^{(2k)}_s f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle=2^{2k}\int_{0}^{1}\norm{Q_t^{(k)} Q^{(k)}_{s/2}Q^{(k)}_{s/2}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:almost orthogonal}}{\lesssim}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\min\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}{\max\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}\norm{ Q_{s/2}^{(k)} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{t}+\int_{t}^{1}\right)\frac{\min\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}{\max\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}\norm{ Q_{s/2}^{(k)} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}.

Let us fix a pair of
q,\,q^{\prime}\in[1,\infty]
so that
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
, and consider first for
\frac{s}{2}\leq t
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\min\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}{\max\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}\norm{ Q_{s/2}^{(k)} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{s/2}{t}\right)^{k}\norm{Q_{s/2}^{(k)}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{s/2}{t}\right)^{k+\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{s/2}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle\leq t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{s/2}{t}\right)^{q^{\prime}k+q^{\prime}\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_{s/2}^{(k)}}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_{s/2}^{(k)}}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

where we used Hölder’s inequality in the third line and the fact that
k+\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}>0
in the last line.

Similarly, consider for
\frac{s}{2}\geq t
,

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{1}\frac{\min\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}{\max\{t,\frac{s}{2}\}^{k}}\norm{ Q_{s/2}^{(k)} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\int_{t}^{1}\left(\frac{t}{s/2}\right)^{k-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}(s/2)^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_{s/2}^{(k)}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}

\displaystyle\leq t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\left(\int_{t}^{1}\left(\frac{t}{s/2}\right)^{q^{\prime}k-q^{\prime}\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{t}^{1}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{s/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\left(\int_{t}^{1}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_{s/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{1}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tQ^{(2k)}_s f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\lesssim\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(s^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q_{s/2}^{(k)} f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\norm{P_{1/2} f}_{L^{p}_{M}},

which implies the desired result.
∎

From the definition of the Besov norm in Definition
1.3
, it is clear
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}
for
\beta>\alpha
. When
M=\mathbb{T}^{n}
, this embedding is compact. The corresponding compact embedding result is not known in our setting, and the following theorem fills this gap.

Theorem 3.13
.

For
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, the embedding
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
for
\beta>\alpha
is compact.

Proof.

It is enough to show that any bounded sequence in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}
admits a Cauchy subsequence in
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
. Take
J\in\mathbb{N}
and recall operator
A_{j}
from Proposition
3.8
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. If
q=\infty
, then by Proposition
3.8
, it holds for any
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,\infty}
that

\displaystyle\sup_{j\geq J}2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{j\geq J}2^{j\beta}2^{j(\alpha-\beta)}\norm{A_j(f) }_{L^{p}_{M}}

(3.6)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{J(\alpha-\beta)}\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\beta}\norm{A_j(f)}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{J(\alpha-\beta)}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,\infty}}.

Similarly, if
q<\infty
, then for any
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq J}2^{\alpha jq}\left(\norm{A_j f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq J}2^{j(\alpha-\beta)q}2^{\beta jq}\left(\norm{A_j f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\right)^{q}

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{J(\alpha-\beta)q}\sum_{j\geq J}2^{\beta jq}\norm{A_jf}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{J(\alpha-\beta)q}\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}}^{q}.

Now, take any bounded sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n}\subset\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}
, which we assume without loss of generality that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\norm{f_n}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}}\leq 1.

By Proposition
3.11
, we see
\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta-d_{h}/p}_{\infty}}<\infty
, hence by Lemma
3.5
,
\norm{P_t f}_{\mathcal{B}^{a}_{\infty}}<\infty
for any
a>0
and
t>0
. Recall
\Theta>0
in (

HC
Θ

). By proposition
3.7
, we see for some
0<\delta<\Theta
,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tf_n}_{\mathcal{C}^{\delta}_{M}}\asymp\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\norm{Q^{(k)}_tf_n}_{\mathcal{B}^{\delta}_{\infty}}<\infty.

Hence, by Arzela-Ascoli (c.f.
[
49
, Theorem 4.43]
), for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence
\{A_{j}(f_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
admits a convergent subsequence in
\mathcal{C}_{M}
, hence in
L^{p}_{M}
for any
p\in[1,\infty]
.

Therefore, one may use the diagonalization argument to see there exists a subsequence
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
so that
A_{j}(g_{n})
converges in
L^{p}_{M}
(in
\mathcal{C}_{b}(M)
if
p=\infty
) for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition, by either (
3.6
) or (
3.7
), for any small
\varepsilon>0
, we may take
J\geq 1
to be sufficiently large so that
2^{J(\alpha-\beta)}<\varepsilon
, since
\alpha<\beta
. Then for
q\leq\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{g_n - g_m}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{0\leq j\leq J}2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(g_n )- A_j(g_m)}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\sup_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\sum_{j\geq J+1}2^{\alpha jq}\norm{A_j(g_n) - A_j(g_m)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{0\leq j\leq J}2^{\alpha j}\norm{A_j(g_n )- A_j(g_m)}_{L^{p}_{M}}+2^{J(\alpha-\beta)}2\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\norm{g_n}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{p,q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{0\leq j\leq J}\norm{A_j(g_n) - A_j(g_m)}_{L^{p}_{M}}+\varepsilon.

Now take
n,m\to\infty
to see the first term on the last line goes to zero, hence

\displaystyle\lim_{n,m\to\infty}\norm{g_n - g_m}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\lesssim\varepsilon,

for any
\varepsilon>0
. In addition, we note that the hidden multiplicative constant is from Proposition
3.8
and it does not depend on
J
. This implies the subsequence
\{g_{n}\}_{\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy in
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
, as desired.
∎

3.6
Connection to the Dirichlet energy

Here we show a connection between the heat semi-group based Besov norm and the Dirichlet form
\mathcal{E}
. It will be useful for obtaining a global solution to (
1.1
) in the presence of a singular energy measure. Let us first introduce a different notion of Besov (semi)norm associated with the heat kernel, which was developed in
[
3
]
.

Definition 3.14
.

For
p\geq 1
and
\alpha\geq 0
, let
f
be a measurable function on
M
, define

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{p,\alpha}:=\sup_{t>0}t^{-\alpha}\left(\iint_{M^{2}}\absolutevalue{f(x) - f(y)}^{p}p_{t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{(}\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\alpha\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
, for any measurable function
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{f}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{p,\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}},

(3.8)

whenever the right hand side is finite.

Proof.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in(0,1]
, recall
q_{k,t}:M^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
from Proposition
2.3
, and let
k>\alpha
. Then for any
p\geq 1
, there is some
c>0
in dependent of
t\in(0,1]
so that

\displaystyle\norm{Q_t^{(k)}f}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{M}\left|\int_{M}q_{k,t}(x,y)f(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\right|^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:locality integral q equals 0}}{=}\left(\int_{M}\left|\int_{M}q_{k,t}(x,y)\left(f(y)-f(x)\right)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\right|^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq: q sub gaussian bound}}{\lesssim}\left(\int_{M}\left[\int_{M}p_{ct}(x,y)\absolutevalue{f(x) - f(y)}\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\right]^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\iint_{M^{2}}p_{ct}(x,y)\absolutevalue{f(x) - f(y)}^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where we used Jensen’s inequality in the last line. Therefore, multiplying both side by
t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}
and take supremum to see the desired result.
∎

Remark 3.16
.
