\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\norm{v^{(t)}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}^{rq}\right]<\infty,

where
\mathbb{E}
is taken over
(\xi,\widetilde{Y}_{0})
.

By Theorem
5.5
,
\nu(\mathfrak{C})=1
and for each
m\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
r\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\norm{\widetilde Y^{:m:}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha m}_{\infty}}^{rq}\right]<\infty.

Since
\widetilde{Y}
is stationary, this implies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\norm{\widetilde Y^{:m:}}_{\mathcal{C}([t,t+1];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha m}_{\infty})}^{rq}\right]<\infty,\qquad 1\leq m\leq n.

Now apply (
5.11
) and Theorem
1.7
on the interval
[t,t+1]

\displaystyle\norm{Y^{:k:}}_{\mathcal{C}([t,t+1];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty})}\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{k}\norm{v^{(t)}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}^{j}\norm{\widetilde Y^{:k-j:}}_{\mathcal{C}([t,t+1];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha(k-j)}_{\infty})}.

Therefore, by Hölder’s inequality and the previous bounds,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\norm{Y^{:k:}}_{\mathcal{C}([t,t+1];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty})}^{q}\right]<\infty.

For
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{Y^{:k:}}_{\mathcal{C}([t,t+1];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty})}\leq\norm{Y^{:k:}}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}}+\norm{Y^{:k:}}_{\mathcal{C}([1,2];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty})}.

The first term has finite
q
-th moment by Definition
5.4
since
\Psi_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
, and the second term is bounded by the estimate for
t\geq 1
(take
t=1
). This proves the lemma.
∎

6
Solution theory for the remainder equation

We now move on to solve the remainder equation (
1.7
). We will show that for appropriate initial conditions, the local (in time) solution to (
1.7
) exists under (
1.8
). If in addition (
1.9
) holds, then the solution to the remainder equation exists for all
t>0
.
For appropriate
v_{0}
, the solution of (
1.7
) is understood as the solution to the integral equation

\displaystyle v_{t}=e^{-t}P_{t}v_{0}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)}P_{t-s}\left(Y^{:k:}(s)v(s)^{n-k}\right)\mathrm{d}s.

(6.1)

For obtaining global solution, we will also need to consider the following mollified remainder equation (see Remark
6.7
):

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}&=-(L+1)v_{\varepsilon}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}Y^{:k:}_{\varepsilon}v^{n-k}_{\varepsilon}\\
v_{\varepsilon}(0,\cdot)&=P_{\varepsilon}\left(v_{0}\right),\end{cases}

(6.2)

which is understood in the mild form

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}(t)=e^{-t}P_{\varepsilon+t}v_{0}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)}P_{t-s}\left(Y_{\varepsilon}^{:k:}(s)v_{\varepsilon}(s)^{n-k}\right)\mathrm{d}s.

6.1
Local well-posedness of the remainder equation

We consider first a slightly more general setting than (
1.7
). Recall
\alpha_{0}=(d_{h}-d_{w})/2
as in (
5.3
) and
n_{0}
as in (
5.4
). Condition (
1.8
) is equivalent to the following:
1\leq n\leq n_{0}
, and there exists
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
so that

\displaystyle\alpha(n-1)<\left(d_{w}-\alpha n\right)\wedge\Theta,

(6.3)

where
\Theta\in(0,1]
is as in (

HC
Θ

). In this subsection, we fix such
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
. For
T\geq 1
, consider the Banach space of time-dependent distributions

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{T}:=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}\left([0,T];\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}\right)

equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\norm{\mathbf Y}_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}:=\max_{k=1,\dots,n}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\norm{Y_{t}^{(k)}}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}},\qquad\mathbf{Y}=\left(Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)}\right)\in\mathcal{G}_{T}.

and set
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{G}_{1}
.
Consider the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v=-(L+1)v+\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}v^{n-k}Y^{(k)},

(6.4)

where
a_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
for
k=0,\dots n
. We will also let

\displaystyle\gamma\in(\alpha(n-1),d_{w}-\alpha n)\cap(0,\Theta).

(6.5)

The following proposition is similar to
[
33
, Proposition 3.2]
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\gamma>0
be as in (
6.5
),
\eta\in(-\frac{d_{w}}{n},\gamma]\backslash\{0\}
, and
K>2
. Denote

\displaystyle B_{K}:=\left\{\left(v_{0},\mathbf{Y}\right)\in\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{G}:\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}+\norm{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq K\right\}.

Then there exists
c>0
,
\kappa>0
, depending only on
\alpha,\eta,n,\gamma
, so that, for
T^{*}=cK^{-1/\kappa}\in(0,1)
, (
6.4
) with initial value
v_{0}
admits a unique solution in the Banach space
\mathcal{S}
of functions
v\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T^{*}],\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}\right)
, for which

\displaystyle\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}}:=\sup_{t\in(0,T^{*}]}\left\{\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}+t^{\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}+t^{-\frac{\eta\wedge 0}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_t}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\right\}<\infty.

(6.6)

Moreover, the solution map

\displaystyle B_{K}\ni\left(v_{0},\mathbf{Y}\right)\mapsto v\in\mathcal{S}

is Lipschitz and
\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}}\leq C\left(\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}+1\right)
, where
C>0
depends only on
\alpha,\eta,n,\gamma
.

Proof.

In this proof, we will denote
\mathcal{S}_{T}
for each
T\in(0,1]
the Banach space of functions
v\in\mathcal{C}\left([0,T];\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}\right)
with the norm

\displaystyle\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}:=\sup_{t\in(0,T]}\left\{\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}+t^{\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}+t^{-\frac{\eta\wedge 0}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_t}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\right\}.

Consider the solution map of (
6.4
) acting on
v\in\mathcal{S}_{T}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{T}\ni v\mapsto\mathbf{\Phi}(v):=\left(e^{-t}P_{t}v_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)}P_{t-s}Y^{(n)}_{s}\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)}P_{t-s}\left(v^{n-k}_{s}Y^{(k)}_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}s\right)_{t\geq 0}.

We will first show
\mathbf{\Phi}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}
. Consider first

\displaystyle\norm{\mathbf\Phi(v)_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\lesssim\norm{P_tv_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}+\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s} \Psi^{(n)}_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s}\pr{v_s^{n-k}Y^{(k)}_s}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s.

(6.7)

Consider first by Lemma
3.5
that uniformly in
t\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s} Y^{(n)}_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(t-s\right)^{-\frac{\gamma+n\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Y^\prk{n}_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha n}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s\overset{\eqref{ineq:regularity of solution restriction general alpha}}{\lesssim}K.

and

\displaystyle\norm{P_tv_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{\eta-\gamma}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small by Proposition
3.7
and Lemma
3.10
, we see from (
6.5
) that for each
k=0,\dots,n-1
,

\displaystyle\norm{v_s^{n-k}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha k+\varepsilon}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{v_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha k+\varepsilon}_{\infty}}\norm{v_s}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}^{n-k-1}\leq\norm{v_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}^{\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}}\norm{v_s}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}^{n-k-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}}.

Hence, by Lemma
3.5
and Theorem
1.7

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}\norm{v^{n-k}_s Y^\prk{k}_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(t-s\right)^{-\frac{\gamma+\alpha k}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_s^{n-k}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha k+\varepsilon}_{\infty}}\norm{Y^\prk{k} }_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq K\int_{0}^{t}\left(t-s\right)^{-\frac{\gamma+\alpha k}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}^{\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}}\norm{v_s}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}^{n-k-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq K\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{n-k}\int_{0}^{t}\left(t-s\right)^{-\frac{\gamma+\alpha k}{d_{w}}}s^{-\Xi_{k}}\mathrm{d}s,

where

\displaystyle\Xi_{k}={\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}-\frac{\eta\wedge 0}{d_{w}}\left(n-k-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)}.

When
\eta\geq 0
, we see for all
k=0,\dots,n-1
and sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,
\Xi_{k}=\frac{\gamma-\eta}{\gamma}\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{d_{w}}<1
by (
6.3
) and
\Xi_{k}=0
when
\eta=\gamma>0
. For
-\frac{d_{w}}{n}<\eta<0
,

\displaystyle\Xi_{k}=\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{d_{w}}-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}(n-k),

which is linear in
k=0,\dots,n-1
. Hence
\max_{k=0,\dots,n-1}\Xi_{k}=\max\{\Xi_{0},\Xi_{n-1}\}
.
Note
\Xi_{0}=\frac{\varepsilon}{d_{w}}-\frac{n\eta}{d_{w}}<1
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, and

\displaystyle\Xi_{n-1}=\frac{\alpha(n-1)+\varepsilon}{d_{w}}-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}\leq\frac{n-1}{2n-1}+\frac{\varepsilon}{d_{w}}<1,

for all
n\geq 1
and sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. Hence uniformly in
t\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle t^{\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s}\pr{v^{n-k}_s Y^\prk{k}_s}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{n-k}Kt^{1+\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}-\left(\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon+\gamma}{d_{w}}+\Xi_{k}\right)}.

Consider first when
0\leq\eta\leq\gamma
, we may pick a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
so that for all
k=0,\dots,n-1

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon+\gamma}{d_{w}}-\Xi_{k}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon+\gamma}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:regularity of solution restriction general alpha}}{>}1-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon+\gamma}{d_{w}}\overset{\eqref{ineq:regularity of solution restriction general alpha}}{>}0.

For
-\frac{d_{w}}{n}<\eta<0
, the exponent is then

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}_{k}:=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon+\gamma}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}+\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}\left(n-k-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{d_{w}}-\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{d_{w}}+\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}+\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}(n-k)-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}-2\frac{\alpha k+\varepsilon}{d_{w}}+\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}(n-k),

which is linear in
k=0,\dots,n-1
. Since
-\frac{d_{w}}{n}<\eta
, we see for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}_{0}=1-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}(n-1)-\frac{2\varepsilon}{d_{w}}>0.

On the other hand, since
\alpha<\frac{d_{w}}{2n-1}
from (
6.5
), we see for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}_{n-1}=1-\frac{2\alpha(n-1)}{d_{w}}-\frac{2\varepsilon}{d_{w}}>1-2(n-1)\frac{1}{2n-1}-\frac{2\varepsilon}{d_{w}}>0.

Denote

\kappa^{\prime}:=\max\left\{\Xi^{\prime}_{k},1-\frac{\alpha n}{d_{w}}:0\leq k\leq n-1\right\},

then by collecting all terms, we see that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T]}t^{\frac{\gamma-\eta}{d_{w}}}\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty}}\lesssim KT^{\kappa^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{k}\right).

(6.8)

Now observe that if
\eta>0
, then
\norm{\mathbf\Phi(v)_t}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{\mathbf\Phi(v)_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}
uniformly in
t\geq 0
, hence we will consider the case for
-\frac{d_{w}}{n}<\eta\leq 0
.
Let us now consider

\displaystyle\norm{\mathbf\Phi\pr{v}_t}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s}Y^\prk{n}_s}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s} \pr{v_s^{n-k} Y^\prk{k}_s }}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\mathrm{d}s.

By Lemma
3.5
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s}Y^\prk{n}_s}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(t-s\right)^{-\frac{\alpha n}{d_{w}}}\mathrm{d}sK\lesssim Kt^{\kappa^{\prime}}.

By a similar argument, we see for
k=0,\dots,n-1
, it holds uniformly in
t\in(0,1]
that

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s} \pr{v_s^{n-k}Y^\prk{k}_s}}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim K\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{n-k}t^{-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(t-s\right)^{-\frac{\alpha k}{d_{w}}}s^{-\Xi_{k}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim Kt^{\kappa^{\prime}}\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{n-k}.

Finally, since
\eta\neq 0
,
t^{-\frac{\eta}{d_{w}}}\norm{P_tv_0}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}\leq K.
Collecting all the terms to see for so me
C_{0}\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\norm{\mathbf\Phi(v)}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}\leq C_{0}\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}+T^{\kappa^{\prime}}C_{0}K\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{k}\right)\leq C_{0}K+T^{\kappa^{\prime}}C_{0}K\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T}}^{k}\right).

(6.9)

We may fix
\ell_{0}\geq 2
and let
R_{0}:=\ell_{0}C_{0}K\geq 2
,
\kappa:=\kappa^{\prime}/n
, and set

T^{*}:=\left(\left(\frac{\ell_{0}-1}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{n}}\ell_{0}C_{0}K\right)^{-\frac{1}{\kappa}}\leq\left(\frac{R_{0}/(C_{0}K)-1}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}R_{0}^{k}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\kappa}}.

Then (
6.9
), along with Proposition
4.10
implies
\mathbf{\Phi}
maps the closed ball of radius
R_{0}
centered at
0
in
\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}
to itself. By a similar argument, one can show
\mathbf{\Phi}
is a contraction on this ball for possibly smaller
T^{*}
.

Finally, to see the solution map is Lipschitz, we let
(v_{0},\mathbf{Y}),(w_{0},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}})\in B_{K}
, and let
v,w\in\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}
be solution to (
6.4
) with initial condition
v_{0},w_{0}
and coefficients
\mathbf{Y},\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}
respectively. Then by construction,
\norm{v}_{\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}},\norm{w}_{\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}}\leq R_{0}
. Now consider

\displaystyle\norm{v_t - w_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{P_t\pr{v_0 - w_0}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}+\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s} \pr{v_s^n -w^n_s}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s}\widetilde{Y}_s^{(k)}\pr{v_s^{n-k} - w_s^{n-k}}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\norm{P_{t-s}w_s \pr{\widetilde Y^{(k)}_s - Y^{(k)}_s}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}\mathrm{d}s,

and use similar decomposition for
\norm{v_t - w_t}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}
. A similar argument shows that the solution map is Lipschitz on the ball of radius
R_{0}
in
\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}
.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
6.1
, for any
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
, there exits a unique local solution to the equations (
1.7
) and (
6.2
). Recall
\mathfrak{C}
as in Definition
5.4
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
and
2\leq n\leq n_{0}
satisfies (
6.3
), take
\gamma>0
be as in (
6.5
) and
\eta
be as in Proposition
6.1
. Suppose
Y
is solution to (
5.1
) with initial condition
Y_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
. Then for each
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\eta}_{\infty}
,

•

There exists a (random)
T^{*}>0
depending only on
\max_{k=1,\dots,n}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\norm{Y^{:k:}_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}}
and
\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}}
, so that equation (
1.7
) admits a unique solution,
v
, in
\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}}
as in (
6.6
).

•

For each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, there exist (random)
T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\geq T^{*}>0
, so that equation (
6.2
) admits a unique solution,
v_{\varepsilon}
, in
\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}
.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\norm{v- v_\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{S}_{T^{*}\wedge T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}}=0.

Remark 6.3
.

Note that (
6.3
) implies
\alpha<\frac{d_{w}}{2n-1}\leq\frac{d_{w}}{n}
. Hence
\eta=-\alpha
satisfies condition of Proposition
6.1
and hence Lemma
6.2
.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.7
) and (
6.2
) is a direct consequence of Proposition
6.1
and Theorem
5.5
. The fact that
T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\geq T^{*}
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
is due to the mollified initial condition and Wick powers of
Y
has smaller Besov norms. Lastly, the convergence is given by the local Lipschitzness of solution map as in Proposition
6.1
.
∎

6.2
Global Solution and Coming Down from Infinity

One main difference between this section and the corresponding sections in
[
91
,
109
]
is that on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, the solution the the remainder equation (
6.1
)
v_{t}
is sufficiently regular and belongs to the domain of the Dirichlet form,
\mathcal{F}
, for
t>0
. This allows them to control the
L^{p}_{M}
norm of
v
in terms of its Dirichlet energy on the torus. In our setting,
v
need not be inside the domain
\mathcal{F}
. However, we will show that running their arguments for the mollified solution
v_{\varepsilon}
is sufficient to produce the desired a priori estimate.

In this subsection, we fix an arbitrary
Y_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
and let
Y
be the solution to (
5.1
) with initial condition
Y_{0}
.
We assume also for the rest of the paper that (
1.9
) holds for some odd
n\geq 3
, which is equivalent to the following condition: for some
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
,

\displaystyle n\alpha<\frac{d_{w}}{2}.

(6.10)

Hence, we shall fix an
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
that satisfies both (
6.10
) and (
6.3
), which is needed in Proposition
6.9
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
, suppose
v_{\varepsilon}
is the local solution to (
6.2
) given by Lemma
6.2
on
[0,T^{*}_{\varepsilon}]
. Then for any small enough
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi(Y_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{C}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{C}_{M}^{\delta},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad v_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{1}}\mathcal{B}^{\frac{d_{w}}{2}+\delta_{2}}_{2,\infty}\qquad\text{ almost surely.}

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, by Lemma
5.3
, we can choose a sufficiently small
\delta\in(0,\Theta)
so that
Y_{\varepsilon}^{:n:}\in\mathcal{C}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\delta}_{M}
for each
n\geq 1
. Note that by Lemma
3.5
,
v_{0,\varepsilon}:=P_{\varepsilon}(v_{0})\in\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}
for each
\beta>0
. Hence by Lemma
3.6
, we may pick an appropriate
\beta>0
so that

\displaystyle t\mapsto P_{t}\left(v_{0,\varepsilon}\right)\in\mathcal{C}_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2d_{w}}}\mathcal{B}^{\frac{d_{w}}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}}_{\infty}.

By Proposition
6.1
and the fact
v_{\varepsilon,0}\in\mathcal{B}^{\delta}_{\infty}
, we see
and
v_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\delta}=\mathcal{C}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{C}_{M}^{\delta}
. Since
C_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{C}_{M}^{\delta}
forms an algebra, we see
\Psi(Y_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{C}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{C}_{M}^{\delta}=\mathcal{C}_{T^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{B}^{\delta}_{\infty}
by Proposition
3.7
. Finally, by Jensen’s inequality and Proposition
4.10
, we see for any
T<T^{*}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\norm{\int_0^\cdot P_{t-s}\Psi_\varepsilon(s)\mathrm ds }_{\mathcal{C}_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2d_{w}}}\mathcal{B}_{2,\infty}^{\frac{d_{w}}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}}}\lesssim\norm{\int_0^\cdot P_{t-s}\Psi_\varepsilon(s)\mathrm ds }_{\mathcal{C}_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2d_{w}}}\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\frac{d_{w}}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}}}\lesssim\norm{\Psi_\varepsilon(s)}_{\mathcal{C}_{T}\mathcal{B}^{\delta}_{\infty}}<\infty.

∎

Lemma 6.5

(Weak formulation)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
, suppose
v_{\varepsilon}
is the local solution to (
6.2
) given by Lemma
6.2
on
[0,T^{*}_{\varepsilon}]
. For any
0\leq t\leq T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{*}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
,

\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(T),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(t),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}=\int_{t}^{T}\left(-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi)-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s,

where
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s):=\Psi\left(Y_{\varepsilon}(s),v_{\varepsilon}(s)\right)
.

Proof.

Set
S_{t}:=e^{-(1+L)t}=e^{-t}P_{t}
and
v_{0,\varepsilon}:=P_{\varepsilon}v_{0}
. The mild formulation (
6.1
) reads

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}(s)=S_{s}v_{0,\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{s}S_{s-r}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad s\in[0,T].

First take
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)
. By the mild formula (
6.1
) at times
T
and
t
,

\displaystyle\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(T)-v_{\varepsilon}(t),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\braket{(S_{T}-S_{t})v_{0,\varepsilon},\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\int_{0}^{t}\braket{(S_{T-r}-S_{t-r})\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{T}\braket{S_{T-r}\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}r.

(6.11)

On the other hand, using
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\braket{S_{u}f,\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}=\braket{S_{u}f,-(L+1)\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}
for
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)
and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s),-(L+1)\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\braket{(S_{T}-S_{t})v_{0,\varepsilon},\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\int_{0}^{t}\braket{(S_{T-r}-S_{t-r})\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{T}\braket{(S_{T-r}-\mathrm{Id})\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}r.

(6.12)

Subtracting (
6.2
) from (
6.2
), we get

\displaystyle\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(T)-v_{\varepsilon}(t),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}=\int_{t}^{T}\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s),-(L+1)\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{T}\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}s.

By Lemma
6.4
and (
2.1
), this is

\displaystyle\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(T),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(t),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}=\int_{t}^{T}\left(-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi)-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s),\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Now let
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
. Define
\varphi_{m}:=P_{1/m}\varphi\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)\subset\mathcal{F}
. By standard semigroup properties of regular symmetric Dirichlet forms,
\varphi_{m}\to\varphi
in
\mathcal{F}
and in
L^{2}_{M}
. Apply the identity above to
\varphi_{m}
.
Passing
m\to\infty
is justified term by term. First,

\displaystyle\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(T)-v_{\varepsilon}(t),\varphi_{m}-\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\to 0,

since
\varphi_{m}\to\varphi
in
L^{2}_{M}
. Next, by Cauchy–Schwarz for the Dirichlet form,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\int_t^T \mathcal E(v_\varepsilon(s),\varphi_m-\varphi)\,\mathrm ds}\leq(T-t)\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),v_{\varepsilon}(s))^{1/2}\,\mathcal{E}(\varphi_{m}-\varphi,\varphi_{m}-\varphi)^{1/2}\to 0,

because
s\mapsto v_{\varepsilon}(s)
is continuous in
\mathcal{F}
on
[t,T]
. Finally,

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\absolutevalue{\int_t^T \braket{\Psi_\varepsilon(s),\varphi_m-\varphi}_{L^2_M}\,\mathrm ds}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\norm{\varphi_m-\varphi}_{L^{2}_{M}}\int_{t}^{T}\norm{\Psi_\varepsilon(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}\,\mathrm{d}s\to 0,

and the integral is finite since, by Lemma
6.4
,
\Psi_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}([t,T];\mathcal{C}_{M}^{\delta})\subset L^{1}([t,T];L^{2}_{M})
. Therefore the same identity holds for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6

(Testing against
v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}
)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
,suppose
v_{\varepsilon}
is the local solution to (
6.2
) given by Lemma
6.2
on
[0,T^{*}_{\varepsilon}]
. Then for any integer
p\geq 2
and
0\leq t\leq T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\left(\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(T)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}\right)=\int_{t}^{T}\left(-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),v_{\varepsilon}^{p-1}(s))-\norm{v_\varepsilon(s)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}\right)\mathrm{d}s.

Consequently, for almost every
t\in(0,T_{\varepsilon}^{*})
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\partial_{t}\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}^{p}_{L^{p}_{M}}=-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(t),v_{\varepsilon}^{p-1}(t))-\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(t),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{2}_{M}}.

Remark 6.7
.

Lemma
6.6
is why we need to look at the mollified equation (
6.2
). Indeed, Proposition
6.1
only gives
v_{t}\in\mathcal{B}^{\eta}_{\infty}
for
\eta\in(0,\Theta)
and
t\in[0,T^{*}]
. But for
\mathcal{E}(v_{t},v_{t})
to make sense, one needs
v_{t}\in\mathcal{B}^{d_{w}/2+\delta}_{2,\infty}
for some
\delta>0
, which need not be true since
0<\Theta\leq 1<d_{w}/2
.

Proof.

Fix
0\leq s\leq s^{\prime}\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{*}
. We start from

\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(s')}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\norm{v_\varepsilon(s)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}=\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime})-v_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime}),v_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime})^{p-1}-v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}.

Applying Lemma
6.5
with
t=s
,
T=s^{\prime}
and test function
\varphi=v_{\varepsilon}(s)^{p-1}\in\mathcal{F}
yields

\displaystyle\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(s')}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\norm{v_\varepsilon(s)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime}),v_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime})^{p-1}-v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{s^{\prime}}\left(-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(r),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s))-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(r),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(r),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r.

Now fix
0\leq t\leq T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{*}
and let
\underline{t}=(t_{0},\dots,t_{m})
be a subdivision of
[t,T]
. Summing the above identity on each interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
gives

\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(T)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\mathfrak{G}(\underline{t})=\mathfrak{J}(\underline{t}),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}(\underline{t}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(t_{i+1}),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t_{i+1})-v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t_{i})}_{L^{2}_{M}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}(\underline{t}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left(-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t_{i}))-\braket{v_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t_{i})}_{L^{2}_{M}}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t_{i})}_{L^{2}_{M}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Using Lemma
6.4
and the same Young-integration argument as in
[
91
,
109
]
, as
|\underline{t}|:=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}|t_{i}-t_{i-1}|\to 0
,

\mathfrak{J}(\underline{t})\to\int_{t}^{T}\left(-\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s))-\norm{v_\varepsilon(s)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}+\braket{\Psi_{\varepsilon}(s),v^{p-1}_{\varepsilon}(s)}_{L^{2}_{M}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Moreover, exactly as in
[
91
, Lemma 3.7]
,

\mathfrak{G}(\underline{t})\to\frac{p-1}{p}\left(\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(T)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}-\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}\right).

Passing to the limit yields the first identity. The map
t\mapsto\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{p}_{M}}^{p}
is therefore absolutely continuous on compact subintervals of
(0,T_{\varepsilon}^{*})
, and differentiating gives the second identity for a.e.
t\in(0,T_{\varepsilon}^{*})
.
∎

To obtain an initial condition independent bound, we need the following elementary result.

Lemma 6.8

(
[
109
, Lemma 3.8]
)
.

Let
\lambda>1
and
f:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be differentiable such that

f^{\prime}(t)+c_{1}f(t)^{\lambda}\leq c_{2}

for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
and all
t\in[0,T]
. Then for all
t>0
,

f(t)\leq\min\left(\frac{f(0)}{(1+tf(0)^{\lambda-1}(\lambda-1)\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}})^{\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}},\left(\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)\leq\min\left(t^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\left((\lambda-1)\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}},\left(\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right).

We now prove an analogue of
[
109
, Proposition 3.7]
for
v_{\varepsilon}
.

Proposition 6.9

(Coming down from infinity)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
, let
v_{\varepsilon}
be the mollified local solution of (
6.2
) on
[0,T_{\varepsilon}^{*}]
.
Then for all
T>0
and sufficiently large odd integer
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C=C(p,T)>0
independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
v_{0}

such that for all
t\in(0,T\wedge T_{\varepsilon}^{*}]
,

\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}\leq C\left(1+t^{-\frac{1}{n-1}}\right)\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\Xi^{\varepsilon}\right)_{k,T}^{q_{k,p}}\right)^{\frac{1}{n-1}},

(6.13)

for some
q_{k,p}>0
depends on
k=1,\dots,n
and
p
, where

\displaystyle\Xi_{k,T}^{\varepsilon}:=\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\norm{Y_\varepsilon^{:k:}(s)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}},\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,1].

As a consequence, (
6.13
) holds true for
v
.

Proof.

Write
\Psi(v,Y)=-v^{n}+\Psi^{\prime}(v,Y)
,
\Psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(s):=\Psi^{\prime}(v_{\varepsilon}(s),Y_{\varepsilon}(s))
, and apply Lemma
6.6
with exponent
2p
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2p}\partial_{t}\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}

\displaystyle+C_{p}\mathcal{E}(v_{\varepsilon}^{p}(t),v_{\varepsilon}^{p}(t))+\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}+\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{n+2p-1}_{M}}^{n+2p-1}

\displaystyle=\braket{\Psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(t),v^{2p-1}_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{2}_{M}}.

The goal is to control
\absolutevalue{\braket{\Psi'_{\varepsilon}(t),v_{\varepsilon}^{2p-1}(t)}_{L^2_M}}_{L^{2}_{M}}
using
K_{t}
and
L_{t}
, where

K_{t}:={\mathcal{E}}(v_{\varepsilon}^{p}(t),v_{\varepsilon}^{p}(t))\text{ and }L_{t}:=\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{n+2p-1}_{M}}^{n+2p-1}.

The triangle inequality gives

\absolutevalue{\braket{\Psi'_{\varepsilon}(t),v^{2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^2_M}}_{L^{2}_{M}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\absolutevalue{\braket{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t),v_\varepsilon^{n-k+2p-1}(t)}_{L^2_M}}_{L^{2}_{M}},

and we will estimate each term in RHS above individually. We now pick a sufficiently small
\delta>0
so that
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-\delta>\alpha_{0}
. By Lemma
3.9
, it holds uniformly in
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
t\in[0,T^{*}_{\varepsilon}]
that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\braket{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t),v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^2_M}}_{L^{2}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}k}_{\infty}}\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}_{1,1}^{\alpha^{\prime}k}}

(6.14)

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{Y^{:k:}_s}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}k}_{\infty}}\norm{v_\varepsilon^{n-k+2p-1}(t)}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha k}_{1,\infty}},

where we used the fact that
\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{1,1}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{1,\infty}}
for every
\beta<\beta^{\prime}
in the last inequality. Recall
\norm{\cdot}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}
in (
1.5
), we see by Lemma
3.10
and (
6.10
)

\displaystyle\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}_{1,\infty}^{\alpha k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{1}_{M}}^{1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}}\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}^{d_{w}/2}_{1,\infty}}^{\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{1}_{M}}^{1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}}\left(\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{1}_{M}}+\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{d_{w}/2}_{1,\infty}}\right)^{\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}},

(6.15)

where we used the fact that
\norm{P_1f}_{L^{p}_{M}}\leq\norm{f}_{L^{p}_{M}}
for all
f\in L^{p}_{M}
in the last inequality.

Since
\mu(M)<+\infty
, we have by Jensen’s inequality,

\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{1}_{M}}=\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{n-k+2p-1}_{M}}^{n-k+2p-1}\lesssim\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{n+2p-1}_{M}}^{n-k+2p-1}=L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}}.

Since
p\geq 2-n
is odd, we can apply Lemma
3.18
and obtain

\norm{v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{d_{w}/2}_{1,\infty}}=\norm{v^{2p+(n-k+1)-2}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\dot{\mathcal{B}}^{d_{w}/2}_{1,\infty}}\lesssim\norm{v^{2p+2(n-k+1)-4}(t)}_{L^{1}_{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
2<q_{0}:=\frac{2d_{h}/d_{w}}{d_{h}/d_{w}-1}
and note that
2p+2(n-k+1)-4=p(2+\frac{4(n-k-1)}{2p})
. Let
p\geq 1
sufficiently large so that
(2+\frac{4(n-k-1)}{2p})\leq q_{0}
and apply Lemma
3.21
and Jessen’s inequality to see

\displaystyle\norm{v^{2p+2(n-k+1)-4}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{1}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\norm{v_{\varepsilon}^p(t)}_{L^{2+\frac{4(n-k-1)}{2p}}_{M}}^{2+\frac{4(n-k-1)}{2p}}\lesssim\left[\norm{v_\varepsilon^p(t)}_{L^{2}_{M}}+K_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]^{2+\frac{4(n-k-1)}{2p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[\norm{v_\varepsilon^p(t)}_{L^{2}_{M}}^{2}+K_{t}\right]^{1+\frac{2(n-k-1)}{2p}}=\left[\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}+K_{t}\right]^{1+\frac{2(n-k-1)}{2p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{n+2p-1}_{M}}^{2p}+K_{t}\right]^{1+\frac{2(n-k-1)}{2p}}\lesssim\left[L_{t}^{\frac{2p}{n+2p-1}}+K_{t}\right]^{1+\frac{2(n-k-1)}{2p}}.

Inserting those estimates into (
6.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\norm{v_\varepsilon^{n-k+2p - 1}}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha k}_{1,\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}\right)}\left(L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}}+\left[L_{t}^{\frac{2p}{n+2p-1}}+K_{t}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(n-k-1)}{2p}}K_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}}

\displaystyle\leq L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}\right)}\left(L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}}+\left[L_{t}^{\frac{2p}{n+2p-1}}+K_{t}\right]^{1+\frac{(n-k-1)}{2p}}\right)^{\frac{\alpha k}{d_{w}}}.

Inserting this back into (
6.14
) gives

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\braket{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t),v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^2_M}}_{L^{2}_{M}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\norm{Y_\varepsilon^{:k:}}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k+\delta}_{\infty}}L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}\right)}\left(L_{t}^{\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}}+\left[L_{t}^{\frac{2p}{n+2p-1}}+K_{t}\right]^{1+\frac{(n-k-1)}{2p}}\right)^{\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}}.

We now want to apply Young’s product inequality alongside Lemma
6.8
. First, we choose
p
large enough such that
(1+\frac{n-k-1}{2p})\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}<1
for each
k=1,\dots,n
, then choose
r_{1}(k),r_{2}(k),
and
r_{3}(k)
so that

\displaystyle 1=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{1}(k)}+\frac{1}{r_{2}(k)}+\frac{1}{r_{3}(k)}\text{ and }

\displaystyle 1>

\displaystyle\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}\right)r_{2}(k),\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}\left(\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}\right)r_{3}(k),\frac{2p+(n-k-1)}{2p}\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}r_{3}(k)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\gamma(k),\gamma^{\prime}(k),\gamma^{\prime\prime}(k).

Indeed, such choices are possible since

\displaystyle\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{n-k+2p-1}{n+2p-1}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}\right)+\frac{2p+(n-k-1)}{2p}\frac{2\alpha k}{d_{w}}=1.

Applying Young’s product inequality to see for each
k\in\set{1,\dots,n}

\absolutevalue{\braket{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t),v^{n-k+2p-1}_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^2_M}}_{L^{2}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k+\delta}_{\infty}}^{r_{1}(k)}+L_{t}^{\gamma(k)}+L_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime}(k)}+K_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(k)}.

Combined, they give us

\frac{1}{2p}\partial_{t}\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}\lesssim-C_{p}K_{t}-L_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\norm{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k+\delta}_{\infty}}^{r_{1}(k)}+L_{t}^{\gamma(k)}+L_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime}(k)}+K_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(k)}\right)

Recall the elementary inequality that for any
c,c^{\prime}>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
, it holds uniformly in
x\geq 0
that
-cx+c^{\prime}x^{\gamma}\lesssim 1
. Applying this to
K_{t}
leaves us with

\frac{1}{2p}\partial_{t}\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}\lesssim 1-L_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\norm{Y^{:k:}_\varepsilon(t)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}}^{r_{1}(k)}+L_{t}^{\gamma(k)}+L_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime}(k)},

which holds uniformly in
t\in[0,T^{*}_{\varepsilon}]
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.
The elementary inequality
x^{\gamma}\leq cx+\delta
with
c<1
allowed to be arbitrarily close to 0 (at the cost of larger
\delta
) applied to
L_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime}(k)}
and
L_{t}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(k)}
gives

\frac{1}{2p}\partial_{t}\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}+cL_{t}\lesssim 1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\Xi^{\varepsilon}\right)_{k,T}^{r_{1}(k)}.

By Jensen, we have
L_{t}=\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{n+2p-1}_{M}}^{n+2p-1}\gtrsim\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{n+2p-1}=\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p(1+\frac{n-1}{2p})}
. Hence

\frac{1}{2p}\partial_{t}\norm{v_\varepsilon(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}+c^{\prime}\left(\norm{v_{\varepsilon}(t)}_{L^{2p}_{M}}^{2p}\right)^{1+\frac{n-1}{2p}}\lesssim 1+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\Xi^{\varepsilon}\right)_{k,T}^{r_{1}(k)}.

We note that the implicit constants for the above inequalities are independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, hence by Lemma
6.8
, we see (
6.13
) holds.

Lastly, we see by Lemma
6.2
that
v_{\varepsilon}(t)\to v(t)
in
L^{\infty}_{M}
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
for each
t\in(0,T^{*}]
. Since
\Xi_{k,T}^{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
for each
1\leq k\leq n
and
T>0
, we see the desired results also hold for
v
.

∎

Corollary 6.10

(Global well-posedness of Remainder Equation)
.

Take
\gamma>0
,
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
,
2\leq n\leq n_{0}
be an odd integer that satisfies the conditions of Lemma
6.2
. Let
Y
be the solution to (
5.1
) with
Y_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
and
v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
. Then there exists a unique solution
\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty})
of equation (
1.7
).

Proof.

Let
T>0
be arbitrary but fixed, and fix
p\geq 2
sufficiently large so that
L^{p}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
(see Proposition
3.11
). Let
\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}:=(Y,Y^{:2:},\cdots,Y^{:n:})
.
Recall the definition of
\Xi_{k,T}
from Proposition
6.9
, and observe that

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_{k,T}^{q_{k,p}}\leq n\norm{\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_n}_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}^{Q}+n\norm{\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_n}_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}^{q},\qquad Q:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}q_{k,p},\,q:=\min_{1\leq k\leq n}q_{k,p}.

Hence, Proposition
6.9
gives an estimate depending only on
\norm{\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_n}_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}
, which provides an a priori estimate on
\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}
. Thus by Lemma
6.2
there exists
T^{*}:=T^{*}(\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}},\norm{\underline{\mathbf Y}_n}_{\mathcal{G}_{T}})>0
and a unique solution up to
T^{*}
in
\mathcal{C}([0,T^{*}];\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty})
of (
1.7
). Use again Lemma
6.2
to construct a solution to (
1.7
) on
[T^{*},(2T^{*})\wedge T]
with initial condition
v_{T^{*}}
which satisfies the same
L^{p}_{M}
norm bound (and hence
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
norm bound) depending again only on
\norm{\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_n}_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}
. We may iterate this process until the whole interval
(0,T]
is covered.
∎

The following corollary is a direct consequence of Proposition
6.9
and Lemma
5.14
. We omit its proof.

Corollary 6.11

(Positive-time moment bound)
.

Under the assumptions of Corollary
6.10
, let
p\geq 1
be as in Proposition
6.9
and denote by
v(\cdot;v_{0})
the solution to (
1.7
) starting from
v_{0}
. Then for every
q\geq 1
,

\sup_{v_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\sup_{0<t<\infty}\left(t^{q/(n-1)}\wedge 1\right)\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\|v(t;v_{0})\|_{L_{M}^{2p}}^{q}\Big]<\infty.

7
The solution as a Markov process and its invariant measure

This section constructs invariant measures for the renormalized
\Phi^{n+1}
dynamics as a process in
\mathfrak{C}
in Definition
5.4
.
We first recall the global solution framework from
[
33
]
, then prove that the associated transition operators form a Feller semigroup, derive a positive-time moment bound, and finally apply the classical Bogoliubov–Krylov theorem (in the style of
[
109
, Proposition 4.4]
) to obtain existence of invariant measures.

Recall
\alpha_{0}
as in (
5.3
) and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
as in (
5.4
). Through out this section, we assume both (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) hold for some odd integer
3\leq n\leq n_{0}
. We shall fix
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
so that (
6.3
) and (
6.10
) holds. As a consequence of Corollary
6.10
, the remainder equation (
1.7
) admits a global solution for any
v\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
.

Definition 7.1

(
[
33
, Definition 3.15]
)
.

Fix an arbitrary
Y_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
. Let
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
and

\gamma>0
and
v\in\mathcal{C}((0,\infty);\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{\infty})
be the solution to (
1.7
) with initial condition
v_{0}=\phi_{0}-Y_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
given by Corollary
6.10
. We define the solution to (
1.1
) with initial condition
\phi_{0}
by

\phi_{t}(\phi_{0}):=v_{t}+Y_{t}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty},\qquad t\geq 0.

Remark 7.2
.

By
[
33
, Proposition 3.16]
, the above definition is independent of the choice of
Y_{0}
.

Lemma 7.3

(
[
33
, Lemma 3.17]
)
.

For every
t>0
,
\phi_{t}\in\mathfrak{C}
almost surely.

For completeness, we first show that this solution defines a Markov process.

Lemma 7.4

(Markov property of
u
)
.

Under the setting of Definition
7.1
, for each
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
let

\phi=\phi(\cdot;\phi_{0})=\{\phi=\phi_{t}(\phi_{0})\}_{t\geq 0}

denote the corresponding global solution with initial condition
\phi_{0}
. For bounded Borel
f:\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
, define

\mathbf{P}_{t}f(v):=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{t}(v)\right)\right],\qquad t\geq 0,\,v\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}.

Then
\{\mathbf{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a Markov semigroup, and for every
s,t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{s+t}(\phi_{0})\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\mathbf{P}_{t}f\left(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0})\right)\qquad\text{a.s.}

(7.1)

Proof.

Fix
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
. By Definition
7.1
, write the corresponding solution as

\phi=Y+v,

where
Y
solves (
5.1
) and
v
solves (
1.7
) globally by Corollary
6.10
.

Fix
s\geq 0
and define the time-shifted noise
\xi^{(s)}
by

\xi^{(s)}(\varphi):=\xi\left(\varphi(\cdot-s,\cdot)\mathbb{1}_{[s,\infty)}(\cdot)\right),\qquad\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times M).

By independent increments of white noise,
\xi^{(s)}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
and has the same law as
\xi
.
Let

\tau_{s}Y_{t}:=Y_{s+t},\qquad\tau_{s}v_{t}:=v_{s+t},\qquad\tau_{s}\phi_{t}:=\phi_{s+t},\qquad t\geq 0.

By the mild formulations of (
5.1
) and (
1.7
),

(\tau_{s}Y,\tau_{s}v)
solves the same renormalized system on
[0,\infty)
, driven by
\xi^{(s)}
, with initial datum

(\tau_{s}Y_{0},\tau_{s}v_{0})=(Y_{s},v_{s})
; hence
\tau_{s}\phi
is a renormalized solution started from
\phi_{s}
.
For
s>0
, Lemma
7.3
gives
\phi_{s}\in\mathfrak{C}
almost surely, so Definition
7.1
applies at time
s
; for
s=0
, the claim is trivial. Therefore, by Corollary
6.10
and independence of the splitting
[
33
, Proposition 3.16]
, for each
t,s>0

\phi_{s+t}(\phi_{0})=\phi_{t}\left(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0});\xi^{(s)}\right)\qquad\text{almost surely,}

where
\phi_{t}(\phi_{0},\eta)
is the solution to (
1.1
) with initial condition
\phi_{0}
and noise input
\eta
.
Now let
f:\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded and Borel. Conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{s}
and using the previous identity,

\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{s+t}(\phi_{0})\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{t}\left(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0});\xi^{(s)}\right)\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}\right].

Since
\xi^{(s)}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
and has the same law as
\xi
, the conditional law of

\phi_{t}(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0});\xi^{(s)})
given
\mathcal{F}_{s}
equals the law of
\phi_{t}(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0});\xi)
. Hence

\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\mathcal{\phi}_{t}\left(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0});\xi^{(s)}\right)\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}\right]=\mathbf{P}_{t}f\left(\phi_{s}(\phi_{0})\right),

which proves (
7.1
).

Taking expectation in (
7.1
), for every
v\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
,

\mathbf{P}_{s+t}f(v)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{P}_{t}f\left(\phi_{s}(v)\right)\right]=\mathbf{P}_{s}\left(\mathbf{P}_{t}f\right)(v),

so
\{\mathbf{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup.
Also
\mathbf{P}_{0}f=f
, so
\{\mathbf{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a Markov semigroup.
∎

Proposition 7.5

(Feller property)
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
7.1
, the Markov semigroup

\{\mathbf{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
from Lemma
7.4
is Feller on

\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
: for every
t\geq 0
and every bounded continuous

f:\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
, the map

\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}\ni v\mapsto\mathbf{P}_{t}f(v)

is bounded and continuous.

Proof.

Recall that by Corollary
6.10
, the renormalized solution to (
1.1
) is pathwise unique and depends continuously on the initial condition in the space
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
for an appropriate
\alpha>0
.
Fix
t\geq 0
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty})
, We note that boundedness is immediate, so it remains to prove continuity. The case
t=0
is trivial, so let
t>0
, and let

v^{(m)}_{0}\to v_{0}
in
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
.

Choose one
Y_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}
and use the same driving noise
\xi
and the same process
Y
for all initial data. Write

\phi(v^{(m)}_{0})=Y+v^{(m)},\qquad\phi(v_{0})=Y+v,

where
v^{(m)}
and
v
solve (
1.7
) with initial conditions

v^{(m)}_{0}-Y_{0}
and
v_{0}-Y_{0}
, respectively.

Since
v^{(m)}_{0}\to v_{0}
in
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
, there exists
R>0
such that

\sup_{m}\|v^{(m)}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\vee\norm{v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\leq R.

Fix
\omega
in a full-probability set where all Wick powers are defined continuously on
[0,t]
.
Set

M_{t}(\omega):=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\norm{Y^{:k:}(s,\omega)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha k}_{\infty}}<\infty.

By Proposition
6.9
(applied to
v
itself) and the embedding used in Corollary
6.10
, there exists a finite random constant
A_{t}(\omega)
, independent of the initial condition, such that

\sup_{0<s\leq t}\sup_{m\geq 1}\norm{v^{(m)}(s,\omega)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\vee\sup_{0<s\leq t}\norm{v(s,\omega)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\leq A_{t}(\omega).

Now apply Proposition
6.1
pathwise on successive time intervals. Since the coefficients are bounded by
M_{t}(\omega)
on
[0,t]
and all restart data are bounded by
A_{t}(\omega)
, there exists

\delta(\omega)>0

(depending only on
A_{t}(\omega)
and
M_{t}(\omega)
) such that the local solution map on any interval of length at most
\delta(\omega)
is Lipschitz in the initial datum, with Lipschitz constant depending only on the same bounds.
Partition
[0,t]
into at most

N(\omega):=\lceil t/\delta(\omega)\rceil

subintervals and iterate the local Lipschitz estimate. This gives

\norm{v^{(m)}(t,\omega)-v(t,\omega)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\leq C_{t}(\omega)\norm{v^{(m)}_0-v_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}},

for some finite
C_{t}(\omega)
independent of
m
. Hence

v^{(m)}(t,\omega)\to v(t,\omega)

and therefore

\phi_{t}(v^{(m)},\omega)\to\phi_{t}(v,\omega)

almost surely.

Because
f
is bounded and continuous,

f(\phi_{t}(v^{(m)}))\to f(\phi_{t}(v))

almost surely, and dominated convergence yields

\mathbf{P}_{t}f(v^{(m)})=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{t}(v^{(m)})\right)\right]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{t}(v)\right)\right]=\mathbf{P}_{t}f(v).

Thus
v\mapsto\mathbf{P}_{t}f(v)
is continuous, as desired.
∎

Corollary 7.6

(Positive-time moment bound for
u
)
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
7.1
, for every

q\geq 1
,

\sup_{\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{q/(n-1)}\wedge 1\right)\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{\phi_t(\phi_0)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]<\infty.

Proof.

Recall
\nu
as in (
5.5
) and fix
q\geq 1
. By
[
33
, Proposition 3.16]
, the law of the solution does not depend on the chosen splitting of initial value. Thus we may choose
Y_{0}\sim\nu
(independent of
\xi
), and write

\phi_{t}(u_{0})=Y_{t}+v_{t},

where
Y
is stationary and
v
solves (
1.7
) with initial condition
\phi_{0}-Y_{0}
.

Choose
p\geq 1
as in Corollary
6.11
, and enlarge
p
if needed so that

\frac{d_{h}}{2p}<\alpha.

Then Proposition
3.11
gives

\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\lesssim\norm{f}_{L^{2p}_{M}}.

Hence Corollary
6.11
implies

\sup_{\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{q/(n-1)}\wedge 1\right)\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]<\infty.

For the linear part, stationarity yields

\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{Y_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{Y_{0}\sim\nu}\Big[\norm{Y_0}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]<\infty,

where finiteness follows from Theorem
5.5
with
n=1
.

Using
\norm{a+b}^{q}\lesssim_{q}\norm{a}^{q}+\norm{b}^{q}
, we get

\left(t^{q/(n-1)}\wedge 1\right)\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{\phi_t(\phi_0)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{Y_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]+\left(t^{q/(n-1)}\wedge 1\right)\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{v_t}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big].

Taking suprema in
\phi_{0}
and
t
gives the claim.
∎

Proposition 7.7

(Existence of invariant measure (Bogoliubov–Krylov))
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
7.1
, the Feller semigroup

\{\mathbf{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
from Lemma
7.4
admits at least one invariant probability measure

\mu_{\star}
on
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
. Moreover,

\mu_{\star}(\mathfrak{C})=1,

so
\mu_{\star}
is supported on
\mathfrak{C}
(by Lemma
7.3
).

Proof.

We follow the Bogoliubov–Krylov strategy as in
[
109
, Proposition 4.4]
.
Pick
\alpha^{\prime}\in(\alpha_{0},\alpha)
, Corollary
7.6
gives, for every
q\geq 1
,

\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\Big[\norm{\phi_t(0)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}}^{q}\Big]<\infty,

where
\phi_{t}(0)
is the solution started from
0
.

Recall that by Theorem
3.13
(with
p=q=\infty
), the embedding

\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}

is compact. For
R>0
, let

B^{\prime}_{R}:=\set{f\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}:\norm{f}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}}\leq R},

and let
K_{R}
be its closure in
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
; then
K_{R}
is compact in
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
by Theorem
3.13
.
Hence Markov’s inequality yields

\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\left(\phi_{t}(0)\notin K_{R}\right)\leq\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\left(\norm{\phi_t(0)}_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}}>R\right)\lesssim_{q}R^{-q}.

Therefore
\{\mathrm{Law}(\phi_{t}(0)):t\geq 1\}
is uniformly tight in
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}
. Hence, the family of probability measures
\{\mu_{T}\}_{T\geq 1}
on
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}
given by

\mu_{T}(A):=\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{T}\int_{1}^{T+1}\mathrm{Law}\left(\phi_{t}(0)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t\in A\right),\qquad T>0.

for any Borel set
A\subset\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}
, is tight. By Prokhorov, this family is compact in the space of probability measures on
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}
, and there exists
T_{n}\uparrow\infty
and a probability measure

\mu_{\star}
on
\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}
such that
\mu_{T_{n}}\Rightarrow\mu_{\star}
.

It remains to prove invariance. Let
s\geq 0
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty})
. By Markov property,

\int_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}}\mathbf{P}_{s}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{1}^{T+1}\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{t+s}(0)\right)\right]\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{T}\int_{1+s}^{T+1+s}\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\phi_{r}(0)\right)\right]\mathrm{d}r.

Hence

\absolutevalue{\int_{\Binf{-\alpha'}} \mathbf P_sf\,\mathrm d\mu_T-\int_{\Binf{-\alpha'}} f\,\mathrm d\mu_T}\leq\frac{2s\norm{f}_{L^{\infty}}}{T}\xrightarrow[T\to\infty]{}0.

Using the Feller property of
\mathbf{P}_{t}
as in Proposition
7.5
,
\mathbf{P}_{s}f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty})
. Passing to the limit along
T_{n}
gives

\int_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}}\mathbf{P}_{s}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\star}=\int_{\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\star},

for all
s\geq 0
,
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty})
. Thus
\mu_{\star}
is invariant.

Finally, by Lemma
7.3
, for every
t>0
and every initial condition
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\infty}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\phi_{t}(\phi_{0})\in\mathfrak{C}\right)=1,

which implies that
\mu_{\star}
is supported on
\mathfrak{C}
.
∎

8
Appendix

8.1
Proof of Lemma
2.1
,
2.2
, Proposition
2.3
and Lemma
2.5

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

Let
a>0
be fixed. Since
\int_{M}g(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=0
, we have

\displaystyle e^{-aL}g(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}p_{a}(x,y)g(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=c_{a}\int_{M}g(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)+\int_{M}\left(p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\right)g(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\left(p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\right)g(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y).

Note
p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\geq 0
and
\int_{M}p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\mu(\mathrm{d}y)=1-c_{a}\in(0,1)
. Hence, by Jensen’s inequality, we see for each
p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-aL}g}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{M}\left(\int_{M}\left(p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\right)g(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\right)^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{M}(1-c_{a})^{p-1}\int_{M}\left(p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\right)\absolutevalue{g(y)}^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq(1-c_{a})\norm{g}_{L^{p}_{M}},

where we used Fubini’s and symmetry of the heat kernel in the last inequality. On the other hand, if
p=\infty
, then

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-aL}g}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in M}\int_{M}\left(p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\right)\absolutevalue{g(y)}\mu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in M}\int_{M}p_{a}(x,y)-c_{a}\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\norm{g}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}

\displaystyle=(1-c_{a})\norm{g}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}.

Finally, note that for all
s>0
,
\int_{M}e^{-sL}g(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=0
. Hence, for
t\geq a
, we see by the semi-group property that

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-tL}g}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle=\norm{e^{-\left\lfloor\frac{t}{a} \right\rfloor a L}e^{-\pr{t-\left\lfloor\frac{t}{a} \right\rfloor a}L}g}_{L^{p}_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq(1-c_{a})^{\left\lfloor\frac{t}{a}\right\rfloor}\norm{g}_{L^{p}_{M}}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Since Diam
(M)<\infty
, we see by (

HKE
f

) that there are
0<a<b<\infty
so that
a<p_{1}(x,y)<b
, hence
p_{1}(x,y)\asymp 1
uniformly in
x,y\in M
. Suppose now
t>0
and consider

\displaystyle p_{1+t}(x,y)=\int_{M}p_{1}(x,z)p_{t}(z,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}z)\asymp\int_{M}p_{t}(z,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}z)=1,

where we used stochastic completeness (
SC
) and the symmetric property of the heat kernel.

To show (

HC
Θ

) holds globally in
t>0
, it is enough to assume
t>1
. Let
g_{x,y}(u):=p_{1}(x,u)-p_{1}(y,u)
for
x,y,u\in M
, then
\int_{M}g_{x,y}(u)\mathrm{d}\mu(u)=0
, and

\norm{g_{x,y}}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\overset{\eqref{ineq:holder_kernel}}{\lesssim}d(x,y)^{\Theta},\qquad\text{ uniformly in }y,z\in M.

Hence by Lemma
2.1
, there exists
c>0
so that the following inequalities holds uniformly in
t>1
and
x,x^{\prime}\in M

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{p_t(x,y) - p_t(x',y)}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\int_M g_{x,x'}(u)p_{t-1}(u,y) \mu\pr{\mathrm du}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-2ct}\norm{g_{x,x'}}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim e^{-2ct}d(x,x^{\prime})^{\Theta}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-ct}\left(\frac{d(x,x^{\prime})}{t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}}\right)^{\Theta}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{w}}{d_{w}-1}}\right)

where we used the fact that
t\geq 1
and
\mathrm{diam}(M)<\infty
in the last line. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.3
.

Suppose
k\geq 1
is an integer, by
[
42
, Theorem 4, Corollary 5]
, we see uniformly in
(t,x,y)\in(0,1]\times M^{2}
that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{t^k\partial_t^k p_t(x,y)}\lesssim t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}}\exp\left(-C\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\frac{d_{w}}{d_{w}-1}}\right).

By stochastic completeness (
SC
), we may use
[
49
, Theorem 2.27]
to differentiate under the integral sign to get
\int_{M}\partial_{t}^{k}p_{t}(x,\cdot)\mathrm{d}\mu=0
for all
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times M.

By
[
85
, Proposition 3]
,
(P_{t})_{t}
forms a Feller semi-group on
\mathcal{C}_{M}
, hence, we may use
[
49
, Lemma 2.27]
to see for any
f\in\mathcal{C}_{M}
and
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times M

\displaystyle Q^{(k)}_{t}f(x)=(t\partial_{t})^{k}P_{t}f(x)=\int_{M}t^{k}\partial_{t}^{k}p_{t}(x,y)f(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)=:\int_{M}q_{k,t}(x,y)f(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y).

On the other hand, using the identity
Q^{(k)}_{t}=(tL)^{k}P_{t}=2^{k}(tL/2)^{k}P_{t/2}P_{t/2}
, we see by Lemma
2.2
, there is some
c>0
so that uniformly in
(t,x,y,y^{\prime})\in(0,\infty)\times M^{3}
with
y,y^{\prime}\leq(t/2)^{1/d_{w}}
,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{q_{k,t}(x,y) -q_{k,t}(x,y')}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{M}\absolutevalue{q_{k,t/2}(x,z)}\absolutevalue{p_{t/2}(z,y) - p_{t/2}(z,y')}\mu(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{M}p_{ct}(x,z)\absolutevalue{p_{t/2}(z,y) - p_{t/2}(z,y')}\mu(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{d(y,y^{\prime})}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\Theta}\int_{M}p_{ct}(x,z)p_{ct}(z,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}z)e^{-ct}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{d(y,y^{\prime})}{t^{1/d_{w}}}\right)^{\Theta}p_{2ct}(x,y)e^{-ct}.

It remains to show (
2.6
) for
t\geq 1
. However, this follows from (
2.5
) and Lemma
2.1
as in the proof of Lemma
2.2
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

Note that uniformly in
t>0
and
x,y\in M
,
p_{t}(x,y)\lesssim 1+t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{1}_{M}\to L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim 1+t^{-d_{h}/d_{w}}.

Then the Riesz–Thorin interpolation theorem, gives for each
p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{\infty}_{M}}\leq\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{1}_{M}\to L^{\infty}_{M}}^{\frac{1}{p}}\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{\infty}_{M}\to L^{\infty}_{M}}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim 1+t^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}p}}.

Apply Riesz–Thorin again to see for
q\geq p
,

\displaystyle\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{q}_{M}}\leq\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{\infty}_{M}}^{1-\frac{p}{q}}\norm{e^{-tL}}_{L^{p}_{M}\to L^{p}_{M}}^{\frac{p}{q}}\lesssim 1+t^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)}.

∎

8.2
Proof of Proposition
3.17

Proof of Proposition
3.17
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be positive and
p\in\mathbb{N}
is odd with
p>n-2
. Then for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{a^{2p+n - 2} - b^{2p+n - 2}}\leq\frac{2p+n-2}{p}\left(\absolutevalue{a}^{p+n-2}+\absolutevalue{b}^{p+n-2}\right)\absolutevalue{a^p - b^p}.

(8.1)

Indeed, if
a,\,b
have the same sign, i.e.
ab\geq 0
, then the inequality holds by the mean value theorem. Suppose
a,b
have different signs, i.e.
ab<0
. Let us assume without loss of generality that
a>0>b=-c
for some
c>0
, then

\displaystyle\left(2+\frac{n-2}{p}\right)\left(\absolutevalue{a}^{p+n-2}+\absolutevalue{b}^{p+n-2}\right)\absolutevalue{a^p - b^p}

\displaystyle=\left(2+\frac{n-2}{p}\right)\left(a^{p+n-2}+c^{p+n-2}\right)\left(a^{p}+c^{p}\right)

\displaystyle\geq a^{2p+n-2}+c^{2p+n-2},

where the equality is due to the fact that
p
is odd and
\absolutevalue{\frac{n-2}{p}}\leq 1
. If
n
is even, then we see (
8.1
) holds. On the other hand, if
n
is odd, then

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{a^{2p+n - 2} - b^{2p+n-2}}=a^{2p+n-2}+c^{2p+n-2},

which also implies (
8.1
) holds. Let
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function, define
v(x):=f(x)^{p}
and
u(x):=|f(x)|^{p+n-2}
for all
x\in M
. Denote
C_{p}:=\frac{2p+n-2}{p}
and consider for any
\alpha\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\norm{f^{2p+n - 2}}_{1,\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t>0}t^{-\alpha}\left(\iint_{M^{2}}\absolutevalue{f(x)^{2p+n - 2} - f(y)^{2p+n - 2}}p_{t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:elementary inequality}}{\leq}C_{p}\sup_{t>0}t^{-\alpha}\iint_{M^{2}}\left(u(x)+u(y)\right)\absolutevalue{v(x) - v(y)}p_{t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{p}\sup_{t>0}t^{-\alpha}\left(\iint_{M^{2}}u(x)p_{t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\iint_{M^{2}}\left(v(x)-v(y)\right)^{2}p_{t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{p}\norm{u^2}_{L^{1}_{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{t>0}\left(t^{-2\alpha}\iint_{M^{2}}\left(v(x)-v(y)\right)^{2}p_{t}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=2C_{p}\norm{u^2}_{L^{1}_{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\norm{v}_{2,2\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we used Hölder’s inequality in the third line. This completes the proof of the first desired inequality. The second inequality follows immediately by
[
3
, Proposition 4.6]
.
∎

8.3
Proof of Lemma
4.4
and Lemma
4.9

To prove Lemma
4.4
, we will first need to state some consequences of our assumptions due to
[
8
, Theorem 4.9]
.

Denote for any measurable function
f
on
M
and open set
B\subset M
the average of
f
on
B
as
f_{B}
, i.e.

\displaystyle f_{B}:=\frac{1}{\mu(B)}\int_{B}f\mathrm{d}\mu.

We say the
Poincare inequality
holds if there exists
\kappa\geq 1
so that the following inequality holds uniformly in
(r,x)\in(0,\infty)\times M
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\int_{B(x,r)}\left(f-f_{B(x,r)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu\lesssim r^{d_{w}}\int_{B(x,\kappa r)}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f).

(
\mathrm{PI}_{d_{w}}
)

Let
E\subset F\subset M
be two non-empty open subsets, we say
\varphi
is a
cutoff function
of
E\subset F
if
\varphi
taking values in
[0,1]

\mu
-almost everywhere,
\varphi\equiv 1
on a neighborhood of
\overline{E}
and
\varphi\equiv 0
on
F^{c}
.

We say the
cutoff Sobolev inequality
(

CS

(

d
w

)

) holds if there exists
C_{S}>0
so that for every
x\in M
and
R,r>0
, there is a cutoff function
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}
for
B(x,R)\subset B(x,R+r)
so that the following inequality holds for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}f^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\varphi,\varphi)\leq\frac{1}{8}\int_{B(x,R+r)\backslash B(x,R)}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)+\frac{C_{S}}{r^{d_{w}}}\int_{B(x,R+r)\backslash B(x,R)}f^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu,

(
\mathrm{CS}(d_{w})
)

where we have identified
\varphi
with its quasi-continuous version. For details about quasi-continuity, we refer the reader to
[
52
]
.

Theorem 8.1

(
[
79
, Theorem 2.8]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
be a strongly local regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}_{M}
whose heat kernel satisfies (
1.3
), then
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
satisfies both the Poincare inequality (

PI

d
w

) and the cutoff Sobolev inequality (

CS

(

d
w

)

) hold.

Remark 8.2
.

See
[
8
]
for the converse of Theorem
8.1
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

We fix arbitrary
x\in M
and
r\in(0,\mathrm{diam}(M)]
in this proof, and denote
\sigma B:=B(x,\sigma r)
for any
\sigma>0
. We will let
\varphi
be the cutoff function for
B\subset 2B
provided by (

CS

(

d
w

)

) with
R=r
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)\cap\mathcal{C}_{M}
and set
\bar{f}:=f-f_{2B}
. By the strong locality of
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
, we see
\Gamma(f,f)=\Gamma(\bar{f},\bar{f})
. Hence, we will work with
\bar{f}
.

By the strong locality of the Dirichlet form, the Leibniz rule gives:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\bar{f},\varphi^{2}\bar{f})=\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\bar{f},\bar{f})+2\varphi\bar{f}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\bar{f},\varphi).

Integrating this over the space, and applying the definition of the generator
L
for
f\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(L)
(noting that
Lf=L\bar{f}
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\bar{f}(Lf)\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{M}L(\overline{f})\varphi^{2}\overline{f}\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}\Gamma\left(\overline{f},\varphi^{2}\overline{f}\right)=\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)+2\int_{2B}\varphi\bar{f}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,\varphi).

Rearranging to isolate the energy of
f
on the left side:

\displaystyle\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)=\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\bar{f}(Lf)\mathrm{d}\mu-2\int_{2B}\varphi\bar{f}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,\varphi).

(8.2)

We bound the two terms on the right-hand side of (
8.2
) in absolute value.
For the first term, applying Young’s inequality (
ab\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}a^{2}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}b^{2}
with
\epsilon=r^{-d_{w}}
), and noting
\varphi\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\bar{f}(Lf)\mathrm{d}\mu\right|\leq\frac{r^{-d_{w}}}{2}\int_{2B}|\bar{f}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu+\frac{r^{d_{w}}}{2}\int_{2B}|Lf|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu.

(8.3)

For the second term, we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the energy measure
\mathrm{d}\Gamma
, followed by the integral Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and finally Young’s inequality in the form
2ab\leq\frac{1}{2}a^{2}+2b^{2}
:

\displaystyle 2\left|\int_{2B}\varphi\bar{f}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{2B}|\bar{f}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\varphi,\varphi)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)+2\int_{2B}|\bar{f}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\varphi,\varphi).

(8.4)

Substituting (
8.3
) and (
8.3
) back into (
8.2
) and subtracting
\frac{1}{2}\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)
from both sides leaves:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)\leq\frac{r^{-d_{w}}}{2}\int_{2B}|\bar{f}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu+\frac{r^{d_{w}}}{2}\norm{Lf}_{L^{2}_{2B}}^{2}+2\int_{2B}|\bar{f}|^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\varphi,\varphi).

(8.5)

By Theorem
8.1
and (

CS

(

d
w

)

), we see

\displaystyle 2\int_{2B}\absolutevalue{\overline{f}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\varphi,\varphi)\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{2B}\varphi^{2}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(f,f)+\frac{2C_{S}}{r^{d_{w}}}\int_{2B\backslash B}\absolutevalue{\overline{f}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu.

Inserting this back into (
8.5
), and unitize the fact that
\varphi
is bounded between
0
and
1
to see the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

For (1): suppose
\alpha\leq\beta
, then

\displaystyle\norm{u}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}=\norm{P_1u}_{L_{M}^{\infty}}+\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(b)}_tu}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\leq\norm{P_1u}_{L_{M}^{\infty}}+\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\beta}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(b)}_tu}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}=\norm{u}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}}.

For (2), we note if
\alpha\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\norm{u}_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty}}=\norm{P_1u}_{L_{M}^{\infty}}+\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{Q^{(b)}_tu}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{u}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}+\sup_{t\in(0,1]}t^{-\frac{\alpha}{d_{w}}}\norm{u}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\lesssim\norm{u}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}.

For (3), we take
\beta\in(0,\alpha\wedge\Theta)
, then

\displaystyle\norm{u}_{L^{\infty}_{M}}\leq\norm{u}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}}\lesssim\norm{u}_{\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty}}\lesssim\norm{u}_{\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\alpha}}.

∎

8.4
Proof of Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.10

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Since for
n\geq 2
, the Wick powers
Y^{:n:}_{\varepsilon}
of
Y_{\varepsilon}
are defined via Hermit polynomials and the space of Hölder continuous functions forms an algebra, it is enough to show both
Y_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\delta_{1}}(\mathcal{C}_{M}^{\delta_{2}})
for some
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
, and the renormalization counterterm
C_{\varepsilon}:M\to\mathbb{R}
given by Definition
5.2
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\delta_{2}}_{M}
for some sufficiently small
\delta_{2}
.

Suppose
Y
is the solution to (
5.1
) with
Y_{0}\in\mathcal{B}^{-\alpha}_{\infty}
. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
and
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times M
,

\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=P_{t}P_{\varepsilon}\left(Y_{0}\right)(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{M}p_{t-s+\varepsilon}(x,y)\xi\left(\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}s\right)=:P_{t}P_{\varepsilon}\left(Y_{0}\right)(x)+\widetilde{Y}_{\varepsilon}(t,x).

Recall for
G_{\varepsilon}:M^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
from (
5.6
). It holds uniformly in
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
y,z,x\in M
that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{ G_\varepsilon(x,y) - G_\varepsilon(x,z)}=

\displaystyle 2\absolutevalue{\int_0^\infty e^{-t} \pr{p_{t+2\varepsilon}(x,y) - p_{t+2\varepsilon}(x,z) }\mathrm dt}

\displaystyle=2\absolutevalue{\int_0^\infty e^{-t} \int_M p_{t}(x,w)\pr{p_{2\varepsilon}(w,y) - p_{2\varepsilon}(w,z)}\mu(\mathrm dw) \mathrm dt}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:holder_kernel}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}-\Theta}d(x,y)^{\Theta}.

Hence uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
and
x,x^{\prime}\in M
,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{C_{\varepsilon}(x) - C_\varepsilon(x')}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{G_\varepsilon(x,x) - G_\varepsilon(x,x') +G_\varepsilon(x,x') - G_\varepsilon(x',x')}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}-\Theta}d(x,x^{\prime})^{\Theta}.

Also, by Lemma
2.2
, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in M}C_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:sgu}}{\lesssim}\int_{0}^{1}(t+2\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t,

which is finite. Hence, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1],\,C_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\frac{\Theta}{2}}_{M}.

Next, we show that for each
\varepsilon>0
and
T>0
, the process
Y_{\varepsilon}
is jointly continuous on
[0,T]\times M
via the Kolmogorov’s continuity lemma. Let
t\in[0,T]
and
x,y\in M
, consider

\displaystyle\norm{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,x) - \widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,x')}_{L^{2p}_{\Omega}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{M}\int_{M}\left(p_{t-s}(x^{\prime},y_{1})-p_{t-s}(x,y_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left(p_{t-s}(x^{\prime},y_{2})-p_{t-s}(x,y_{2})\right)p_{\varepsilon}(y_{1},y_{2})\mu(\mathrm{d}y_{1})\mu(\mathrm{d}y_{2})\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{M}\left(p_{t-s+\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},y_{1})-p_{t-s+\varepsilon}(x,y_{1})\right)\left(p_{t-s}(x^{\prime},y_{1})-p_{t-s}(x,y_{1})\right)\mu(\mathrm{d}y_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}p_{2(t-s)+\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})-2p_{2(t-s)+\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},x)+p_{2(t-s)+\varepsilon}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:holder_kernel}}{\lesssim}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{d(x,x^{\prime})}{(t-s+\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{d_{w}}}}\right)^{\Theta}(t-s+\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim d(x,x^{\prime})^{\Theta}\varepsilon^{1-\frac{d_{h}+\Theta}{d_{w}}}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,x) - \widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t',x)}_{L^{2p}_{\Omega}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\int_{M}\left(p_{t^{\prime}-s+\varepsilon}(x,y)-p_{t-s+\varepsilon}(x,y)\right)\left(p_{t^{\prime}-s}(x,y)-p_{t-s}(x,y)\right)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}p_{2(t^{\prime}-s)+\varepsilon}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(p_{2(t^{\prime}-s)+\varepsilon}(x,x)-2p_{t+t^{\prime}-2s+\varepsilon}(x,x)-p_{2(t-s)+\varepsilon}(x,x)\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}p_{2(t^{\prime}-s)+\varepsilon}(x,x)\mathrm{d}s.

Let us call the two terms on the right hand side
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
respectively. We have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}(2t-2s+\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}-1}\mathrm{d}s\absolutevalue{t-t'}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\absolutevalue{t-t'}

and

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:sgu}}{\lesssim}\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}(2t-2s+\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\varepsilon^{-\frac{d_{h}}{d_{w}}}\absolutevalue{t-t'}.

Therefore, by triangular inequality,

\displaystyle\norm{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,x) -\widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t',x')}_{L^{2p}}\lesssim\left(d(x,x^{\prime})^{\frac{\Theta}{2}}+\absolutevalue{t-t'}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\varepsilon^{-\frac{d_{h}}{2d_{w}}}.

Hence by the Kolmogorov’s continuity lemma (c.f.
[
81
, Theorem 1.1]
), we see for all
q\geq 1
and sufficiently small
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
,

\displaystyle\norm{ \sup_{(t,x),\, (t',x') \in[0,T]\times M \atop(t,x) \neq(t',x') } \frac{\abs{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,x) - \widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t',x')}}{\abs{t-t'}^{2\delta_1} + d(x,x')^{2\delta_2}}}_{L^{q}_{\Omega}}\lesssim 1.

(8.6)

In addition, we see from
[
84
, Theorem 11.18]
that for each
T>0
,
q\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\norm{\sup_{(t,x) \in[0,T]\times M} \widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,x)}_{L^{q}_{\Omega}}\lesssim 1.

(8.7)

Hence, we see from a sequence of elementary inequalities that for any
\varepsilon>0
,
q\geq 1
and
T>0
, there are sufficiently small (and possibly different)
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0

\displaystyle\norm{\norm{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon}_{\mathcal C_T^{\delta_1} \mathcal C_M^{\delta_2}} }_{L^{q}_{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=\norm{\sup_{t \in[0,T]} \norm{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,\cdot)}_{\mathcal C_M^{\delta_2}} +\sup_{t,t' \in[0,T]\atop t \neq t'} \frac{\norm{ \widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t,\cdot) - \widetilde Y_\varepsilon(t',\cdot)}_{\mathcal C_M^{\delta_2}}}{\abs{t-t'}^{\delta_1}}}_{L^{q}_{\Omega}}<\infty.

Finally, we see from Proposition
3.7
, for
\delta_{2}\in(0,\Theta)
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{\norm{\widetilde Y_\varepsilon}_{\mathcal C_T \mathcal B^{\delta_2}_{\infty}}}_{L^{q}_{\Omega}}<\infty.

Finally, by Lemma
3.5
and Lemma
3.6
, we see for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
P_{t}P_{\varepsilon}\left(Y_{0}\right)\in\mathcal{C}^{\delta_{1}}\mathcal{C}^{\delta_{2}}_{M}
. Collecting all terms above to see the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.10
.

We split the integral at
s=u
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(u)=\int_{0}^{u}s^{-a}(u+s)^{-b}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{u}^{1}s^{-a}(u+s)^{-b}\mathrm{d}s=:\mathrm{I}_{1}(u)+\mathrm{I}_{2}(u).

For
0<s\leq u
, we have
u<u+s\leq 2u
so
(2u)^{-b}\leq(u+s)^{-b}<u^{-b}
. Hence uniformly in
u\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{1}(u)\asymp u^{-b}\int_{0}^{u}s^{-a}\mathrm{d}s\asymp u^{1-b-a}.

Now for
u\leq s\leq 1
, we have
s\leq u+s\leq 2s
, so
(2s)^{-b}\leq(u+s)^{-b}\leq s^{-b}
. Hence uniformly in
u\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{2}(u)\asymp\int_{u}^{1}s^{-b-a}\mathrm{d}s\asymp\begin{cases}\absolutevalue{1-u^{1-a-b}},&\text{ for }a+b\neq 1,\\
\log\left(1/u\right),&\text{ for }a+b=1.\end{cases}

Collecting all terms above we see

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(u)=\mathrm{I}_{1}(u)+\mathrm{I}_{2}(u)\asymp\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if }a+b<1,\\
1+\log(1/u),&\text{ if }a+b=1,\\
u^{1-a-b},&\text{ if }a+b>1,\end{cases}

which is the desired result.
∎
